## AI Research

### [Not Pinning Your OpenRouter Provider Might Invalidate Your Research](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KsyoSAyBRXtwzSugg/not-pinning-your-openrouter-provider-might-invalidate-your)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span>Please share this with anyone doing AI research with 3rd party providers so that they can ensure their research won’t be corrupted.</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>When you ask </span><a href="https://openrouter.ai/"><span>OpenRouter</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflgfyf1k6av"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlgfyf1k6av">[1]</a></sup></span><span> to give you tokens from a given model, OpenRouter sends your request to a random available provider.</span></li><li value="2"><span>OpenRouter providers have variable quality. Ensuring that your provider is high quality is really difficult. </span></li><li value="3"><span>There is precedent for an AI safety paper accepted to NeurIPS having its core results entirely overturned by these issues.</span></li><li value="4"><span>A </span><a href="https://claude.ai/code/artifact/6ba2006d-e72e-47b8-9e81-fe6270f8305e"><span>review</span></a><span> of influential AI Safety research codebases that use OpenRouter for their reported results found that 31/32 (97%) of them use OpenRouter unsafely.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefcn1svs4rzkw"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fncn1svs4rzkw">[2]</a></sup></span></li><li value="5"><span>Researchers who wish to do research using OpenRouter or similar providers should take precautions to minimize the risks to their research,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzmg91j9o7ri"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzmg91j9o7ri">[3]</a></sup></span><span> though the current selection of providers is insufficient for ideal scientific reliability.</span></li><li value="6"><span>Replicators should test whether results hold up when these bugs are fixed.</span></li><li value="7"><span>Core AI Safety codebases should make fixes so that downstream users are able to implement best practices.</span></li></ul><p><i><span>This is a tangent I took for a few days during the </span></i><a href="https://www.pivotal-research.org/fellowship"><i><span>Pivotal AI Safety Research Fellowship</span></i></a><i><span>. I’m doing an AI Control project mentored by Adam Kaufman and James Lucassen of </span></i><a href="https://www.redwoodresearch.org/"><i><span>Redwood Research</span></i></a><i><span> and partnered with Aniruddh Pramod.</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2razp7vj9c8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2razp7vj9c8">[4]</a></sup></span><i><span> </span></i></p><p><i><span>This blog post is a human-written summary/analysis of a pile of information requested by Matthew and discovered by Claude.</span></i></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>If you want to see Claude’s findings, they are at </span></b><a href="https://github.com/AMindToThink/openrouter_reliable_research_search"><b><span>this GitHub repo</span></b></a><b><span>.</span></b></li><li value="2"><b><span>Explore Claude’s investigations into the OpenRouter-induced data quality issues of 35 influential AI Safety code repos in this </span></b><a href="https://claude.ai/code/artifact/6ba2006d-e72e-47b8-9e81-fe6270f8305e"><b><span>artifact</span></b></a><b><span>.</span></b></li><li value="3"><b><span>See how different providers stack up against each other </span></b><a href="https://claude.ai/code/artifact/7de484cf-83ab-4e8f-a5a6-4e9d88c27067"><b><span>in this Claude Artifact</span></b></a><b><span>.</span></b></li></ul><h1><span>OpenRouter Kills an AI Safety NeurIPS Paper</span></h1><p><span>In </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jHnZzicKzczkCCArK/r1-cot-illegibility-revisited"><i><span>R1 CoT illegibility revisited</span></i></a><i><span> </span></i><span>from April 2026</span><i><span>,</span></i><span> nostalgebraist revisited the findings in the NeurIPS 2025 paper </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.27338"><span>Reasoning Models Sometimes Output Illegible Chains of Thought</span></a><span> by Arun Jose. It turned out that even when using an inference provider with the </span><i><span>same quantization</span></i><span> (fp8, “as far as [nostalgebraist] could tell”), different providers serve different models with drastically different capabilities and chain-of-thought legibilities. Arun </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jHnZzicKzczkCCArK/r1-cot-illegibility-revisited?commentId=xvC2mb9ZLCoKzPhpa"><span>conceded</span></a><span>:</span></p><p><span>“I think this is pretty convincing re your original argument that my results were contaminated by bad inference setups, and that correspondingly my paper doesn't substantiate some of its claims and conclusions with good evidence.”</span></p><p><span>While trying to set up our research codebase for an AI Control project</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnw97wzlfa2o"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnw97wzlfa2o">[5]</a></sup></span><span> so that it would be easy to perform many experiments and know what led to the results, I came across the curious fact that different OpenRouter model providers serve models at different quantization levels. Looking into it further, it turns out that OpenRouter has barely any quality control, and providers are free to reduce quality to achieve lower costs. This stunned me, since I had always assumed that if you ask for model X from OpenRouter, OpenRouter has to give you responses from model X. That is essentially not true. My colleagues at the Pivotal Fellowship were similarly surprised.</span></p><h1><span>Background</span></h1><p><span>OpenRouter’s providers can:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Use different quantizations</span></li><li value="2"><span>Use different inference backends.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Handle your request parameters differently.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Have different maximum input lengths</span></li><li value="5"><span>Be maliciously finetuned to push agendas or have </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/sleeper-agents-training-deceptive-llms-that-persist-through-safety-training"><span>hidden backdoors</span></a></li></ul><p><span>Some people have discussed these issues before but there is evidently not enough awareness, since 31/32 searched AI Safety codebases that report results from OpenRouter, including recent ones in active development, open themselves up to data quality issues. Though OpenRouter describes this behavior in its documentation, most researchers are unaware it is potentially corrupting their results.</span></p><h2><span>Prior work/writing on the subject.</span></h2><p><span>This is just a selection. For more prior work see Claude’s </span><a href="https://github.com/AMindToThink/openrouter_reliable_research_search/blob/master/reports/prior-work.md"><span>Report</span></a><span>.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19537"><span>The Silent Hyperparameter</span></a><span> </span><ul><li value="1"><span>“...the choice of backend alone can shift benchmark scores by up to 16.6 percentage points and induce high rates of output disagreement”</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Epoch AI’s “</span><a href="https://epoch.ai/gradient-updates/why-benchmarking-is-hard"><span>Why benchmarking is hard</span></a><span>”</span><ul><li value="1"><span>“The selection of an appropriate provider has the biggest impact on model performance”</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.15799"><span>Investigating the Impact of Quantization Methods on the Safety and Reliability of Large Language Models</span></a><ul><li value="1"><span>“Our results show both PTQ [post-training quantization] and QAT [quantization-aware training] can degrade safety alignment”</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2512.09549"><span>Chasing Shadows: Pitfalls in LLM Security Research</span></a></li><li value="5"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.20247"><span>Model Equality Testing: Which Model Is This API Serving?</span></a></li><li value="6"><a href="https://simonwillison.net/2025/Aug/15/inconsistent-performance/"><span>Open weight LLMs exhibit inconsistent performance across providers</span></a></li><li value="7"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jHnZzicKzczkCCArK/r1-cot-illegibility-revisited"><span>R1 CoT illegibility revisited</span></a></li></ul><p><span>Image from </span><a href="https://x.com/xlr8harder/status/1974859337387651125"><span>https://x.com/xlr8harder/status/1974859337387651125</span></a><span>:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/4a5106f5ff4f02ac3a933e90721cb8c5da7cf30b748de3a8d1001ee19f74c843/mu8bhnvlruiusgwmokfv" /><h2><span>No Guarantees from Fixing a Provider</span></h2><p><span>Although selecting a provider cuts down on a major source of variation, it does not guarantee that each time you request the provider for a model, you get the same model.</span></p><p><span>I had Claude look through a large number of model providers and figure out the extent to which they report what they serve, how it has changed over time, and their notice policy for changes in the future. </span><a href="https://github.com/AMindToThink/openrouter_reliable_research_search/blob/4c0f96ded675e48bd5cfc5b63960964ecd77bdab/reports/provider-transparency.md"><span>All 10 audited had gaps</span></a><span>. Fixing a provider is still the best practice available when using 3rd party providers, but unfortunately it falls short of ideal.</span></p><h2><span>No Guarantees From Large N</span></h2><p><span>You might hope that provider-routing would introduce some noise but that as long as the researcher had a large enough sample size and reported the standard deviation, then the research results would still be meaningful/replicable. Unfortunately, this is not necessarily the case. OpenRouter does not assign providers purely randomly. For example, if a provider goes down, your requests will be sent to the remaining providers. </span><b><span>There are no guarantees of performance or of randomness. Researchers should assume that OpenRouter behaves </span></b><i><b><span>adversarially</span></b></i><b><span>. </span></b><span>It is possible that if you ask for GPT-OSS-120B on Monday to do your baseline run, you’ll get a dumb/quantized/truncated model, and if you ask for GPT-OSS-120B on Tuesday to do your experimental run, you’ll get a smarter model, and your intervention will seem to have helped.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefc01nx5qacwk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnc01nx5qacwk">[6]</a></sup></span></p><h2><span>No Guarantees from “The Benchmark Didn’t Move”</span></h2><p><span>OpenRouter has and is continuing to develop an “</span><a href="https://openrouter.ai/docs/guides/routing/auto-exacto"><span>Auto Exacto</span></a><span>” “...step that automatically optimizes provider ordering for all requests that include tools. It runs by default on every tool-calling request, requiring no configuration.” I consider it to be an insufficient measure.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.09141"><span>Accuracy is Not All You Need</span></a><span> find that “ the behavior of compressed models as visible to end users is often significantly different from the baseline model, even when accuracy is similar”</span></li><li value="2"><span>It only applies to tool-calling requests. If the research doesn’t involve tool-calling, then it doesn’t apply</span></li><li value="3"><span>OpenRouter says that Auto Exacto depends on quality data on benchmarks they are running, but that this information is not yet public. While this is a good step towards quality control, it is notably not the same as actually ensuring the models being provided are the same. If OpenRouter started checking that the logprobs are allclose to a trusted reference implementation, I would be appeased.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5bepcur4vfm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5bepcur4vfm">[7]</a></sup></span></li><li value="4"><span>In my opinion, Auto Exacto is not good enough for reliable, replicable, trustable science.</span></li></ul><h2><span>No Guarantees from “Choose the Most Expensive Provider”</span></h2><p><span>It would be great if we could just select the most expensive endpoint to get the one which is most faithfully served. Unfortunately, that is not the case. We found that for `meta-llama/llama-3.3-70b-instruct`, originally in bf16, the most expensive endpoint is in fp8, a cheaper one exists in bf16, and the cheapest is once again in fp8. This also hints that even within a fixed quantization, price doesn’t perfectly track quality.</span></p><h2><span>No Guarantees from “Set a floor”</span></h2><p><span>Some research codebases we reviewed set a quantization floor. Although this can protect them from the most lossily-compressed int4 models, it doesn’t protect them from the great variability which can exist within a higher quantization level.</span></p><h2><span>When is Default OpenRouter Fine?</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><span>The model only has one provider (e.g. proprietary models from OpenAI or Anthropic). Version drift is still a risk.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Exploratory work where significant output variance doesn’t matter much</span></li><li value="3"><span>You want a cheap, high-throughput model with only minimal quality control</span></li></ul><p><span>If you are measuring capabilities, comparing models, harnesses, or setups, measuring subtle propensities or safety behaviors, generating training data, or need reproducibility, default OpenRouter is not fine.</span></p><h1><span>Developing Best Practices</span></h1><p><span>Even with all of this, there is no way to actually be able to rely on providers. But researchers have limited alternative options, and self-hosting is too expensive and cumbersome for most research.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Grab my </span><a href="https://github.com/AMindToThink/openrouter_reliable_research_search/blob/master/skill/use-openrouter-safely/SKILL.md"><span>Claude Skill</span></a><span> to have Claude handle it</span></li><li value="2"><span>Pin the endpoint. Although this isn’t a guarantee, making two requests to the same model of the same provider on the same day probably gives you the same model both times… probably.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>If the provider is down, make switching to a new provider a conscious choice. You’d be testing a new organism, and comparisons with the old provider may or may not hold.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffhmoq7d3elw"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfhmoq7d3elw">[8]</a></sup></span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>Pin the quantization, just in case the provider decides to switch their quantization</span></li><li value="4"><span>Record the provider that served the response, and include in your paper/codebase the same way you’d report temperature.</span></li><li value="5"><span>Set allow_fallbacks to false.</span></li><li value="6"><span>Ensure that the provider’s context limits work for your use case, or you may face truncation.</span></li><li value="7"><span>Consider doing all of your experiments in a short period of time. This eliminates the chances that a provider will change how they function between experiments.</span></li><li value="8"><span>Set “require_parameters: true” so it is less likely the provider ignores your parameters (temperature, top_p, etc)</span></li><li value="9"><span>Remember to set data_collection if you’re doing something you’d prefer to stay private.</span></li><li value="10"><span>Setting ‘seed’ doesn’t guarantee determinism, sadly. This isn’t really the providers’ fault, since </span><a href="https://thinkingmachines.ai/blog/defeating-nondeterminism-in-llm-inference/"><span>ensuring determinism is really hard</span></a><span>. Keep this in mind.</span></li><li value="11"><span>Consider benchmarking your providers at the start of your research, to ensure quality. Though this isn’t really sufficient because </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.09141"><span>Accuracy is Not All You Need</span></a><span>.</span></li></ul><h1><span>Spotting </span><a href="https://github.com/AMindToThink/openrouter_reliable_research_search/blob/master/findings/fingerprints.json"><span>Fingerprints</span></a></h1><p><span>Nostalgebraist’s experience with different model providers allowed him to see signs of a degraded model in Arun Jose’s CoT legibility results. Identifying the effects of provider degradation on a model is not always possible for a few reasons:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Most of us aren’t Nostalgebraist</span></li><li value="2"><span>The idea behind techniques like quantization are exactly that they allow the provider to degrade quality without users noticing</span></li><li value="3"><span>If a paper publishes aggregated results without transcripts or metadata logs, there is much less information to indicate a bad inference</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7bvy14tepvb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7bvy14tepvb">[9]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span>If you want to attempt detective work, there are </span><a href="https://github.com/AMindToThink/openrouter_reliable_research_search/blob/master/findings/fingerprints.json"><span>some things you could attempt</span></a><span>. If there are transcripts available, you can check for token soup, special tokens where they don’t belong, and bad formatting (eg for reasoning tokens). There might be subtle disconnects between what the researchers say they did (eg set a temperature) and what they actually got (maybe the model provider didn’t respect the temperature parameter. Did the temperature impact the outputs as expected?).</span></p><p><span>The absence of visible fingerprints of bad inference is very weak evidence that the inference was done properly, and this type of detective work should not be relied upon.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/47fb6ea875f526f828d26562e901e000557b846c78ed48388a1aeedfda0a1177/u8phav1pze2zism1zdpt" /><p><span>Claude’s attempt at a taxonomy of fingerprints.</span></p><h1><span>Highlighting Some Impacted Work</span></h1><p><span>Exploring the 35 AI Safety repos Claude found that mention “OpenRouter”, 34 called OpenRouter and 32 use OpenRouter for their reported results. 31 out of 32 (all except nostalgebraist) were vulnerable to corruption. It would require deep knowledge of the results and setups to say whether the findings were actually impacted. See the </span><a href="https://claude.ai/code/artifact/6ba2006d-e72e-47b8-9e81-fe6270f8305e"><span>Artifact</span></a><span> to explore Claude’s findings for yourself.</span></p><p><span>Here are some highlights, linked to the line in which a model is called without a provider.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://github.com/UKGovernmentBEIS/inspect_ai/blob/main/src/inspect_ai/model/_openai.py#L352-L363"><span>Inspect AI</span></a></li><li value="2"><span>Redwood Research’s </span><a href="https://github.com/redwoodresearch/basharena_public/blob/main/src/policies/attack_policy.py#L82"><span>BashArena</span></a><span> and LinuxArena </span><a href="https://github.com/linuxarena/control-tower/pull/1091"><span>Control Tower</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefs94hvi6wbq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fns94hvi6wbq">[10]</a></sup></span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://github.com/METR/inspect-tasks-public/blob/37530479012b899833bf704695d09671bb15e7eb/re_bench/nanogpt_chat_rl/ai_rd_nanogpt_chat_rl/sandbox/assets/evaluation.py#L82"><span>METR Re-Bench</span></a></li><li value="4"><a href="https://github.com/subhashk01/oversight-scaling-laws/blob/main/wargames/utils.py#L25"><span>Scaling Laws for Scalable Oversight</span></a></li></ul><h1><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/KsyoSAyBRXtwzSugg/39aa5913b5207bd52acb64209ca255c6e0956ce1a40315f8a1f938e111cee1e6/t5xl6stvqovrcexxwqrd" /></h1><h1><span>Should The Community Do Something About This?</span></h1><h2><span>Fixing Repos</span></h2><p><span>Some important repos upstream of AI Safety research such as Redwood’s BashArena and Control Tower and </span><a href="https://claude.ai/code/artifact/6ba2006d-e72e-47b8-9e81-fe6270f8305e"><span>others</span></a><span> do not support best practices. Maintainers of these repositories should update them ASAP. I’ve already made a </span><a href="https://github.com/linuxarena/control-tower/pull/1091"><span>PR to Control Tower</span></a><span>.</span></p><h2><span>Replicating Research</span></h2><p><span>Like nostalgebraist, we should work to identify which important work has the potential for its results to have been affected by this bug. Claude Fable or GPT-Sol could probably get a long way to fixing and replicating these results autonomously, and I encourage someone to do so (if you are working on OpenRouter-reliability replications please make a </span><a href="https://github.com/AMindToThink/openrouter_reliable_research_search/issues"><span>GitHub Issue</span></a><span> on my Repo to coordinate!).</span></p><h2><span>A New Org?</span></h2><p><span>Even with all of these workarounds, we still have to place a lot of trust in the model providers which they frankly haven’t earned. It is impossible to rule out that even with all of my suggested precautions in place, an individual provider, when asked for tokens, routes some requests to one model and some to another. There is no FDA or SEC for model providers that ensure that their marketing claims are true.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefe5cg1l5l66t"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fne5cg1l5l66t">[11]</a></sup></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefyg4teop3l2d"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnyg4teop3l2d">[12]</a></sup></span><span> A provider can change how they serve their models and not give any notice or warning. Imagine the confusion and sunk time that would occur if </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ksn5yrsC3Wg"><span>two chemicals with the same chemical formula had radically different properties</span></a><span>, only one of which explodes.</span></p><p><span>The only way to be safe from these issues is if you run the models locally, yourself. Then you can report your setup for reproducibility and criticism. But that’s a huge tradeoff. OpenRouter-provided models are usually much cheaper and easier to work with than having to manage your own infrastructure. They also allow for much greater parallelism, which is invaluable for quickly running most research (e.g. evaluations and control research).</span></p><p><span>The AI Safety Community should consider creating an organization to provide standardized access to models. Researchers should be able to call on it to provide the same model, provided the same way, every time it is called (unless explicitly requested by the researcher). Until the AI Safety scientific community figures out best practices and sets up good shared infrastructure, we will have difficulty escaping our ignorance.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlgfyf1k6av"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflgfyf1k6av">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Although I primarily looked into OpenRouter, I am under the impression that other multi-provider routers such as Requesty, Vercel AI Gateway, Unify, and Martian suffer from the same issue. Single-provider hosts don’t have switching and avoid some, but not all, of the problems, making them similar to “pinning a provider”.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fncn1svs4rzkw"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefcn1svs4rzkw">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>By ‘safely’, we mean that the data produced by experiments calling that model are trustworthy in the sense that the request really was made as the researcher sent it.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnzmg91j9o7ri"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefzmg91j9o7ri">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Other things to keep in mind: data_collection defaults to ‘allow’, so be careful before sending proprietary information in requests. And keep an eye on which providers include </span><a href="https://openrouter.ai/docs/guides/best-practices/prompt-caching"><span>caching</span></a><span> for decreased cost and increased speed. This is not the focus of this post, but it is worth mentioning.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2razp7vj9c8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2razp7vj9c8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Thank you to Ethan Nguyen for giving me some feedback on this post.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnw97wzlfa2o"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnw97wzlfa2o">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The project is on extending AI Control protocols with “interrogations.” That means things like asking the untrusted model follow-up questions about why it took suspicious actions and making monitoring or control decisions informed by the answers. In future development, we plan to investigate more complex but similar protocols such as debate.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnc01nx5qacwk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefc01nx5qacwk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Nostalgebraist’s work is great. In his logs we can see that runs of GPQA use multiple providers of varying quality for QwQ-32B. From a single config, the provider mix ratios range from </span><a href="https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nostalgebraist/cot_legibility/f4437e4609770eb6d3def5f30951595c26420613/streamlit_runs/20251019_184002_qwq_gpqa/inference.json"><span>90 Nebius + 10 DeepInfra</span></a><span> for one run to </span><a href="https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nostalgebraist/cot_legibility/f4437e4609770eb6d3def5f30951595c26420613/streamlit_runs/20251017_192243_qwq_gpqa/inference.json"><span>85 DeepInfra + 15 Nebius</span></a><span> for another!</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5bepcur4vfm"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5bepcur4vfm">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I wonder what OpenRouter’s incentives are. To what extent is OpenRouter aligned with users, and in particular, with researchers? I encourage economists to study and </span><a href="https://assets.ctfassets.net/xrfr7uokpv1b/yF0AXklHQd7K3SqKICNTM/e9f9167d1b3c7cce56ab3b8c4cc572da/Palantir_-_Institutional_Sovereignty_in_the_Age_of_AI.pdf"><span>think</span></a><span> about this.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfhmoq7d3elw"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffhmoq7d3elw">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I spend most of my time begging Claude “please please please fail loudly don’t secretly hide bugs”. If your provider is down, you should know.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7bvy14tepvb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7bvy14tepvb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Recording the providers can be really helpful here. I spent a little bit of time trying to find particular research results that are potentially impacted by bad inference. Because </span><a href="https://speechmap.substack.com/p/debuting-speechmap-index-and-lab"><span>SpeechMap</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://raw.githubusercontent.com/xlr8harder/speechmap-data/refs/heads/main/responses/us_hard_meta-llama_llama-3.3-70b-instruct.jsonl"><span>records</span></a><span> their providers, we can tell that their results come from a variety of model providers, and their results on how models deal with controversial speech mixes together model variance and provider variance. In their case, this may be intentional/the right decision if they want to measure how models behave in “real world OpenRouter usage.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fns94hvi6wbq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefs94hvi6wbq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I’m making </span><a href="https://github.com/linuxarena/control-tower/pull/1091"><span>PRs</span></a><span> to fix some of these issues in Control Tower when they are relevant to my research.</span></p><p><span>AI Control dynamics would likely not be affected, but safety/usefulness frontiers might be.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fne5cg1l5l66t"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefe5cg1l5l66t">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Like Cerebras’ “</span><a href="https://inference-docs.cerebras.ai/models/overview#:~:text=We%20are%20committed%20to%20serving%20the%20original%20models%20for%20all%20existing%20endpoints%2C%20without%20modification"><span>We are committed to serving the original models for all existing endpoints, without modification</span></a><span>”. Sounds great, and I’ll prefer them going forward. Still, who knows if that’s true?</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnyg4teop3l2d"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefyg4teop3l2d">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Someone should be keeping a closer eye on model providers. OpenRouter says “[benchmark] data will be shown publicly soon but is not yet available on the site”. Until they do, someone else could step in. But this doesn’t eliminate the possibility providers are serving backdoored models or models that </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.09141"><span>maintain accuracy but are different</span></a><span> in other ways.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KsyoSAyBRXtwzSugg/not-pinning-your-openrouter-provider-might-invalidate-your#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Pseudpocalypse](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eaxoBCmQd4vew9ZXX/pseudpocalypse)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Here's a conjecture: If you put any significant amount of text on the internet under different names, those identities can be linked using only the text itself. This is possible (I conject) because of the statistical "fingerprint" you leave in everything you write.</span></p><p><span>Imagine a website where you can paste in some brand-new text someone just wrote. In return, the website provides links to all the text that writer has ever published under any name. It's not perfect, but it's pretty good. As far as I know, no such website exists—at least not on the public internet. But I suspect it's possible and will soon become easy. This will pose some difficulty for pseudonymous blogging.</span></p><p><i><span>Note</span></i><span>: I wrote most of this essay in mid-2025, after which I idiotically sat on it for a year tinkering with theorem statements that none of you will read.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-1">[1]</a></sup></span><span>In the meantime, LLMs have gotten much better at guessing authors from text. (Given the first 1000 words of a draft of this post, Claude 4.8 knows it's me.) Still, I think we're just getting started. I expect to see increasingly obscure writers identified from increasingly small bits of text. I expect that this work even when people are writing in a different register or about unrelated subjects. And I expect that everything I've ever written under any pseudonym will soon be linked to my genuine-nym.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-2">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>A stronger conjecture is that we're heading towards a sort of generalized pseudpocalypse. Perhaps, in the future, if you interact with the world through essentially </span><i><span>any</span></i><span> high-bandwidth channel, then you identify yourself. Say you wear a mask in public and only speak by sub-vocalizing into a voice changer. That's fine, you'll still be identified using your body shape, gait, or chemical signature. Or say you don't like your car being tracked everywhere, so you stop carrying a phone and you somehow convince lawmakers to ban license plates. No problem, your car will still be tracked using tiny scratches or unique pinging sounds from the engine. Or say you don't like being tracked on the internet, so you lock down your browser profile, buy stuff only with Monero, and connect through a chain of three VPNs. That's OK. You'll still be identified through how you wiggle your finger as you scroll down the page. We're all just too unique, and the information theoretic limit is coming for us.</span></p><h2><span>Starting bits</span></h2><p><span>Let's start from first principles. Imagine that at birth, everyone is assigned a random binary string. Whenever you post anything on the internet, you're required to sign it with that string. If the strings are very short, like </span><code><span>0110</span></code><span>, then lots of other people will have the same one as you. But if the strings are very long, then yours would almost certainly be unique and it would be trivial to link all your pseudonyms.</span></p><p><span>Where's the transition point? If you only know that the author is currently alive and living somewhere in the Anglosphere, it's around 29 bits. That's because if there are K digits, then there are 2ᴷ possible binary strings, and if K = 28.86, then 2ᴷ ≈ 490,000,000 is the number of currently-alive Anglosphere-dwellers. If the strings have fewer than 29 bits, then someone else will probably share your string. If they have more than 29 bits, then your string is probably unique.</span></p><p><span>We don't (yet?) have to sign the things we write with immutable government-issued strings. But the way you write still provides lots of clues about you by way of your tone, personality, word choice, and so on.</span></p><p><span>Theoretically speaking, I think it </span><i><span>has</span></i><span> to be possible to link the identities of anyone who writes enough. Imagine again that everyone is assigned a random binary string at birth, but instead of you needing to sign the stuff you write with your string, each time you write a word, there's some chance that a random bit from your string is revealed and added as a signature to your message. For example, maybe a signature of </span><code><span>bit[129]=1</span></code><span> is added, indicating that your string at position 129 has value 1.</span></p><p><span>Think of your string as representing all your writing style quirks, and a bit being revealed as representing when you write something that reveals a preference. For example, maybe bit 18 indicates if you prefer to write your em-dashes with hideous spaces — like this — or without spaces—like this. If you use an em-dash, that bit is revealed.</span></p><p><span>So imagine you've written a lot under Pseudonym A, enough that the full bit-string has been revealed. Maybe it's this:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><pre class="code-block"><span>Pseudonym A: </span><br /><span>110000001111001101110000100001</span><br /><span>010100100101011110111001101000</span><br /><span>100111110010101001101010111010</span><br /></pre></pre><p><span>Now say you start writing under Pseudonym B. Initially, none of the bits will be known:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><pre class="code-block"><span>Pseudonym B: </span><br /><span>??????????????????????????????</span><br /><span>??????????????????????????????</span><br /><span>??????????????????????????????</span><br /></pre></pre><p><span>But slowly, you'll start to leak a few bits:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><pre class="code-block"><span>Pseudonym B:</span><br /><span>?????00???1????1????????1??0??</span><br /><span>?????01??1?????????11????01???</span><br /><span>???1??????1???10??????????????</span><br /></pre></pre><p><span>And eventually you'll leak a lot of bits:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><pre class="code-block"><span>Pseudonym B:</span><br /><span>???0?00?1?11??110??1????10?0??</span><br /><span>?10?001?0101?11???11100?101???</span><br /><span>???11?1??010??10?1?01??011????</span><br /></pre></pre><p><span>Now think about this from the perspective of an "attacker" who wants to know if A and B are the same person. Let's assume they've </span><i><span>only</span></i><span> seen the above bits, and have no information about anyone else. Then here's what the attacker knows:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>A and B have revealed K overlapping bits, which all match.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Different people have a 50% chance of matching on any given revealed bit.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Non-different people have a 100% chance of matching on any given revealed bit.</span></li><li value="4"><span>There are 490,000,000 people.</span></li></ol><p><span>Intuitively, if K was 5, then the fact that all bits match wouldn't prove much, since with 490 million people, lots of people would match on those bits by chance. But if K was 70, it's extremely unlikely that two different people would share all of them, even with such a gigantic pool to start with. It turns out that if there are N other people with random bits, and you pick K of your bits, the probability that </span><i><span>someone</span></i><span> exists who matches all of them is 1 - (1-2⁻ᴷ)ᴺ. When N is 490 million, that looks like this:</span></p><img alt="one_sided_thought_experiment.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784820017/lexical_client_uploads/vspk1pwgpf8m8mjzw87v.svg" /><p><span>Look at that, 29 appears again. (Isn't math wonderful?) In general, the transition happens around whatever number of bits K makes 2ᴷ ≈ N, namely K = log₂(N).</span></p><p><span>If you reveal significantly fewer than 29 bits under pseudonym B, then it's almost guaranteed that there's someone else out there who matches all of them. But if you reveal significantly more than 29 bits, then there's almost no chance that anyone else exists who matches all of them. So the attacker essentially knows that A and B are the same person. And I stress again: They know that without needing to see anything from the other 490 million people.</span></p><p><span>Of course, we don't literally leak bits of immutable feature strings as we write. But you can make the model more realistic, and the same issue persists. If you want to reflect that text only provides noisy information about the writer, then you can add noise to the bits before they're revealed. If you want to reflect that some writing styles are more common than others, then you can make the distribution over bit strings non-uniform. If you want to reflect that certain quirks are more obvious than others, you can give different bits different probabilities of being revealed. All these make the math more complicated. But they don't change the basic conclusion: If your writing style contains at least 29 bits of information, and you do enough writing, you're done.</span></p><p><span>That's my argument that pseudpocalypse is possible. But I don't just want to claim that it </span><i><span>could</span></i><span> happen, eventually. I think it is </span><i><span>likely</span></i><span> to happen, soon, and that the amount of text you need to reveal isn't very large. To make that argument, we need to get specific: What features do people have that are reflected in their writing? How many bits of information do those features contain? How accurately can those bits be guessed from written text?</span></p><p><i><span>Note</span></i><span>: To avoid this turning into a giant information theory lecture, I'll mostly use words like "bit" and "information" without being 100% fully precise about what they mean. I'm doing that because I expect that most people reading this aren't definition-of-bit fetishists, and anyway being hyper-technical would obscure the big picture. If you're an information theory enthusiast and/or skeptical that I know what I'm doing, I refer you to the Section For Skeptical Information Theory Enthusiasts, below. Until then, use your intuition and have faith.</span></p><h2><span>Feature space</span></h2><p><span>Say you knew nothing about me other than that I wrote the above words. And say you had to guess my age or religion or occupation. You could </span><i><span>guess</span></i><span>, right? It wouldn't be perfect, but you'd do much better than you would </span><i><span>without</span></i><span> being able to read those words. Thus, somehow, those words contain information about my demographic characteristics. So I tried to make a list of similar things that you could plausibly guess from text at least somewhat better then chance. Here's what I came up with:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Age</span></li><li value="2"><span>Education</span></li><li value="3"><span>Ethnicity</span></li><li value="4"><span>Family status</span></li><li value="5"><span>Income</span></li><li value="6"><span>Marital status</span></li><li value="7"><span>Mental health</span></li><li value="8"><span>Native language</span></li><li value="9"><span>Occupation</span></li><li value="10"><span>Physical health</span></li><li value="11"><span>Political leanings</span></li><li value="12"><span>Region</span></li><li value="13"><span>Religious affiliation</span></li><li value="14"><span>Sex</span></li></ul><p><span>In the same spirit, if you only read the above words, could you guess how extroverted or conscientious I am? Again, not perfectly. (When I meet people who read this blog, they usually seem surprised I can survive direct sunlight.) But still, I'm sure you'd do OK. So, again, these words contain information about my personality.</span></p><p><span>What features does personality have? The </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HEXACO_model_of_personality_structure"><span>HEXACO model</span></a><span> lists six, namely honesty-humility, emotionality, extraversion, agreeableness, conscientiousness, and openness to experience. I suspect those can all be guessed with reasonable accuracy from a long-enough writing sample. But could you guess more? For each of those six factors, the HEXACO model lists four "facets". In the abstract, trying to guess 6 × 4 = 24 different personality features from text sounds ludicrous, but just look at them:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Honesty-humility</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Sincerity</span></li><li value="2"><span>Fairness</span></li><li value="3"><span>Greed avoidance</span></li><li value="4"><span>Modesty</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Emotionality</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Fearfulness</span></li><li value="2"><span>Anxiety</span></li><li value="3"><span>Dependence</span></li><li value="4"><span>Sentimentality</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>Extraversion</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Social self-esteem</span></li><li value="2"><span>Social boldness</span></li><li value="3"><span>Sociability</span></li><li value="4"><span>Liveliness</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span>Agreeableness</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Forgivingness</span></li><li value="2"><span>Gentleness</span></li><li value="3"><span>Flexibility</span></li><li value="4"><span>Patience</span></li></ul></li><li value="5"><span>Conscientiousness</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Organization</span></li><li value="2"><span>Diligence</span></li><li value="3"><span>Perfectionism</span></li><li value="4"><span>Prudence</span></li></ul></li><li value="6"><span>Openness to experience</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Aesthetic appreciation</span></li><li value="2"><span>Inquisitiveness</span></li><li value="3"><span>Creativity</span></li><li value="4"><span>Unconventionality</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>If you think about specific people, I think you can convince yourself that these 24 represent real things, and that it's plausible to guess them from text. (Your favorite existential angst + science blogger, for example, might score lower on "modesty" than the other honesty-humility facets.) The different sub-factors are surely correlated, but not perfectly correlated.</span></p><p><span>Of course, the biggest thing you learn from people's writing is </span><i><span>how they write</span></i><span>. Do they tend to pointlessly split infinitives? Do they use hyphen-connected words? Do they, incorrectly, position their adverbial clauses?</span></p><p><span>The idea of attributing authorship using writing style features goes back to at least 1440, when Lorenzo Valla demonstrated that the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donation_of_Constantine"><span>Donation of Constantine</span></a><span>—in which Emperor Constantine supposedly donated the Roman Empire to the Catholic Church—used a vernacular that came from 400 years after Constantine's death and was therefore a forgery. In 1851, Augustus De Morgan observed that average word length tends to be stable for the same author. The first "modern" attempt seemingly came in 1964, when Mosteller and Wallace published </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.2307/2283270"><i><span>Inference in an Authorship Problem</span></i></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>This study [attempts] to solve the authorship question of </span><i><span>The Federalist</span></i><span> papers; […]</span></p><p><span>Word counts are the variables used for discrimination. Since the topic written about heavily influences the rate with which a word is used, care in selection of words is necessary. The filler words of the language such as </span><i><span>an</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>of</span></i><span>, and </span><i><span>upon</span></i><span>, and, more generally, articles, preposition, and conjunctions provide fairly stable rates, whereas more meaningful words like </span><i><span>war</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>executive</span></i><span>, and </span><i><span>legislature</span></i><span> do not.</span></p><p><span>After an investigation of the distribution of these counts, the authors execute an analysis […] based on Bayesian methods. The conclusions about the authorship problem are that Madison rather than Hamilton wrote all 12 of the disputed papers.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Get that? The idea is that your usage of the word </span><i><span>war</span></i><span> depends mostly on if you happen to be talking about war. But your usage of </span><i><span>upon</span></i><span> mostly depends mostly on how much you like the word </span><i><span>upon</span></i><span>. To demonstrate this, they took 48 papers written by Hamilton and 50 by Madison and made this table of how many times they used </span><i><span>by</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>from</span></i><span>, and </span><i><span>to</span></i><span>:</span></p><img alt="mosteller.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784820108/lexical_client_uploads/xcqogzluyjrtuflklb8c.png" /><p><span>Madison liked </span><i><span>by</span></i><span>. Hamilton was more a </span><i><span>to</span></i><span> man. Using these kinds of statistics, they concluded that the disputed Federalist papers must have been written by Madison.</span></p><p><span>So I did some research looking for other writing style features that are believed to be stable when people write about different subjects. I found that there are a lot. There were so many that I struggle to even organize them into meaningful groups:</span></p><p><b><span>Low-level frequencies:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Word lengths</span></li><li value="2"><span>Sentence lengths</span></li><li value="3"><span>Paragraph lengths</span></li><li value="4"><span>Punctuation frequencies (commas, colons, dashes, parentheses)</span></li><li value="5"><span>Function word frequencies (</span><i><span>the</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>of</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>and</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>to</span></i><span>)</span></li><li value="6"><span>Adverb frequencies</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Intensifiers (</span><i><span>very</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>really</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>quite</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>pretty</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>so</span></i><span>)</span></li><li value="2"><span>Evidential markers (</span><i><span>apparently</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>evidently</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>obviously</span></i><span>)</span></li><li value="3"><span>Downtoners (</span><i><span>somewhat</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>fairly</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>rather</span></i><span>)</span></li></ul></li><li value="7"><span>Pronoun usage</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Overall preferences (</span><i><span>I</span></i><span>/</span><i><span>we</span></i><span> vs. </span><i><span>you</span></i><span> vs. </span><i><span>he</span></i><span>/</span><i><span>she</span></i><span>/</span><i><span>they</span></i><span>)</span></li><li value="2"><span>Third-person singular preferences (</span><i><span>he</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>she</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>he or she</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>they</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>one</span></i><span>)</span></li></ul></li><li value="8"><span>Modal verbs (</span><i><span>can</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>could</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>might</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>must</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>should</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>will</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>would</span></i><span>)</span></li><li value="9"><span>Hedges (</span><i><span>perhaps</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>maybe</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>possibly</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>probably</span></i><span>)</span></li><li value="10"><span>Conjunctions (</span><i><span>and</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>but</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>yet</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>so</span></i><span>)</span></li><li value="11"><span>Known stable ratios (</span><i><span>the</span></i><span>/</span><i><span>a</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>this</span></i><span>/</span><i><span>that</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>these</span></i><span>/</span><i><span>those</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>I</span></i><span>/</span><i><span>me</span></i><span>/</span><i><span>my</span></i><span>)</span></li><li value="12"><span>Character N-grams (3-grams and 4-grams)</span></li><li value="13"><span>Word N-grams (often 3-grams)</span></li></ul><p><b><span>Lexical features:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Vocabulary size</span></li><li value="2"><span>Lexical diversity / type-token ratio (Number of distinct words divided by number of words.)</span></li><li value="3"><span>Frequencies of rare words</span></li><li value="4"><span>Semantic density</span></li><li value="5"><span>Discourse marker positions, combinations (</span><i><span>So</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>anyway</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>so anyway</span></i><span>)</span></li><li value="6"><span>Use of abbreviations and acronyms</span></li><li value="7"><span>Preference for latinate vs. germanic words (</span><i><span>The</span></i><span> </span><i><span>majestic</span></i><span> </span><i><span>creature</span></i><span> </span><i><span>traversed</span></i><span> </span><i><span>the</span></i><span> </span><i><span>terrain</span></i><span> vs. </span><i><span>the</span></i><span> </span><i><span>mighty</span></i><span> </span><i><span>beast</span></i><span> </span><i><span>strode</span></i><span> </span><i><span>across</span></i><span> </span><i><span>the</span></i><span> </span><i><span>land</span></i><span>.)</span></li></ul><p><b><span>Syntactic features:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Syntactic complexity</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Subordination index</span></li><li value="2"><span>Average parse tree depth</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Use of passive voice.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Nominalization (</span><i><span>She was shocked I ate the pizza</span></i><span> vs. </span><i><span>My pizza consumption shocked her</span></i><span>)</span></li><li value="4"><span>Verb tense and aspect (</span><i><span>I walk</span></i><span> vs </span><i><span>I walked</span></i><span> vs </span><i><span>I was walking</span></i><span> vs </span><i><span>I have walked</span></i><span>)</span></li><li value="5"><span>Sentence structure preferences:</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Branching preferences (Cursed </span><i><span>everyone had a good time when Alice taught some cool dogs I met and brought to dinner to juggle</span></i><span> vs. clumsy-but-readable </span><i><span>I met some dogs and they were cool and I took them to dinner and Alice taught them to juggle and and everyone had a good time</span></i><span>.)</span></li><li value="2"><span>Adverbial clause positioning (</span><i><span>Suddenly I was hungry</span></i><span> vs. </span><i><span>I was, suddenly, hungry</span></i><span> vs. </span><i><span>I was hungry, suddenly</span></i><span>)</span></li><li value="3"><span>Sentence-final weight (</span><i><span>Your plan won't work because of the dyslexic bears</span></i><span> vs. </span><i><span>Dyslexic bears mean your plan won't work.</span></i><span>)</span></li></ul></li><li value="6"><span>Polysyndeton (</span><i><span>I like dogs, cats, and ferrets</span></i><span> vs. </span><i><span>I like dogs and cats and ferrets</span></i><span>.)</span></li><li value="7"><span>Repetition / breaking of syntactic structures.</span></li></ul><p><b><span>Style features:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Register / formality.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Patterns in sentence length (long/short/long/short vs. long/long/short/short)</span></li><li value="3"><span>Stressed syllable interval preferences (e.g. iambic vs. trochaic)</span></li></ul><p><b><span>Rule preference features:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Minor punctuation (</span><i><span>I laughed—you cried</span></i><span> vs. </span><i><span>I laughed — you cried</span></i><span>, "…" (three periods) vs. "…" an actual ellipsis)</span></li><li value="2"><span>Capitalization. (Job titles, seasons, after a colon, mistakes)</span></li><li value="3"><span>Apostrophes (</span><i><span>Steve Jobs' car</span></i><span> vs </span><i><span>Steve Jobs's car</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>1990's</span></i><span> vs </span><i><span>1990s</span></i><span>)</span></li><li value="4"><span>Hyphenation (</span><i><span>a highly-stable feature</span></i><span> vs </span><i><span>a highly stable feature</span></i><span>)</span></li><li value="5"><span>Oxford commas.</span></li><li value="6"><span>Article omissions (</span><i><span>Local dog was petted.</span></i><span> vs. </span><i><span>A local dog was petted.</span></i><span>)</span></li><li value="7"><span>Relative pronoun omissions (</span><i><span>the dog you petted</span></i><span> vs. </span><i><span>the dog that you petted</span></i><span>)</span></li><li value="8"><i><span>Who</span></i><span> vs. </span><i><span>whom</span></i><span>.</span></li><li value="9"><span>Split infinitives (</span><i><span>To obsessively blog</span></i><span> vs. </span><i><span>to blog obsessively</span></i><span>)</span></li></ul><p><b><span>Idiosyncratic features:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Whitespace habits.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Spelling errors (</span><i><span>loose</span></i><span> instead of </span><i><span>lose</span></i><span>)</span></li><li value="3"><span>Grammar errors. (</span><i><span>Between you and I</span></i><span>)</span></li><li value="4"><span>Consistent, unique typos</span></li><li value="5"><span>Other consistent errors (repeated words, un-closed parentheses)</span></li></ul><p><span>That's a lot. There are surely more. And these are all "shallow" features that humans came up with using our tiny little brains. I strongly suspect that there are many more "deep" features that could be found by looking for statistical patterns in a sufficiently large dataset. Many of those features might not even have a coherent English-language description. But they're still there, providing bits for those who seek them.</span></p><p><span>So we leak information about lots of different stuff when we write. But how much information? Is it possible to say how many words are needed to uniquely fingerprint someone?</span></p><p><span>No. To a first approximation, the answer is no. But to a second approximation, maybe? Within an order of magnitude? I'll try, but it's going to be hard.</span></p><h2><span>Demographic bits</span></h2><p><span>How many bits of identifying information does text provide by way of demographic features like age and sex and so on?</span></p><p><span>At first glance, this seems a perilous question, as it depends on the number of categories you consider those things to have. Take sex. For pseudpocalypse purposes, your opinion about how sex should be defined or how many sexes exist is irrelevant. Finer categorizations always provide more information, and our de-pseudonymizing attacker friends will use that information if they can. However, going beyond two categories for sex makes little difference, because the additional categories will be hard to guess and even if you could, categories with low prevalence don't contribute much extra information.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-3">[3]</a></sup></span><span>So, for us, two categories is the right answer.</span></p><p><span>And what about age? At first glance, converting age into a set of categories seems meaningless. If you code age by the millisecond, then there are 3.156 trillion categories for people born in the last 100 years. If you code age by the decade, there are only 10. Here, the thing to notice is that while you might be able to guess my decade of birth from how I write, you don't have a snowball's chance in hell of guessing the millisecond. (See what I did there? People born in certain decades are more likely to use expressions like </span><i><span>snowball's chance in hell</span></i><span>?</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-4">[4]</a></sup></span><span>) If we took age to have some crazy number of categories, we'd have to discount later to reflect the difficulty of guessing. My intuition is that it would be hard to guess age more accurately than around five years, so 20 categories seems reasonable.</span></p><p><span>Following this kind of logic, I chose a number of categories for each of the demographic variables, trying to hit the upper end of what could be guessed from text. (I'll provide the actual categories below.)</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Feature</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Number of categories</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Age</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>20</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Education level</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>6</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Ethnicity</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>6</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Family status</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>2</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>23</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Physical health</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>3</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Political leanings</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>3</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Region</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>23</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Religious affiliation</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>3</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Sex</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>2</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>If each of the age bins were equally likely, then knowing what bin someone fell into would provide 4.32 bits of information, because 2ᴷ ≈ 20 when K = 4.32. Doing that same calculation for each feature gives the maximum amount of information they could contain.</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Feature</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Number of categories</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Maximum bits</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Age</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>20</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>4.32</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>23</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>4.52</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Physical health</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>3</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.58</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Political leanings</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>3</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.58</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Region</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>23</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>4.52</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Religious affiliation</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>3</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.58</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Sex</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>2</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Total</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>32.88</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>But there's a problem. There are more people aged 30-35 than there are people aged 90-95. So, even if you could guess those age bins perfectly, they'd provide less than 4.32 bits of information on average. However, it turns out that categories need to get pretty damned uneven before information content drops very much. A perfectly balanced 50/50 distribution provides 1 bit of information, but if you switch to a 60/40 distribution, you still get 0.971 bits, and you need to go almost to 90/10 before information content drops to 0.5 bits.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-5">[5]</a></sup></span><span>The same basic thing is true when there are more than two categories.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-6">[6]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>So I went through all those features, rated them by how unevenly people are distributed, and tried to discount the bits accordingly. I've put the full details of what the original categories are and how I discounted them in a footnote.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-7">[7]</a></sup></span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Feature</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Number of categories</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Maximum bits</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Estimated bits</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Age</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>20</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>4.32</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>3.9</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Education level</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>6</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>2.58</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>2.1</span></p></td></tr><tr><td
