class="table-cell"><p><span>11</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>3.46</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>2.5</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Marital status</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>3</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.58</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.2</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Mental health</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>3</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.58</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.9</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Native language</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>2</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.6</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Occupation</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>23</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>4.52</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>4.0</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Physical health</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>3</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.58</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.3</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Political leanings</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>3</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.58</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.5</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Region</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>23</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>4.52</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>3.5</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Religious affiliation</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>3</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.58</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.5</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Sex</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>2</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Total</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>32.88</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>26.5</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>But there's another problem. Female 65 to 70 year-old Asians living in Scotland tend to have different {occupations, family statuses, religious affiliations} than 15 to 20 year-old Latinos living in Southeast Australia. That is, the above features are correlated. So as you look at more of them, they gradually become less surprising and thus contribute less information.</span></p><p><span>How much less? Answering that the right way would require us to estimate how likely someone is to fall into each of the 20 × 6 × 6 × 2 × 11 × 3 × 3 × 2 × 23 × 3 × 3 × 23 × 3 × 2 = 8,144,737,920 joint categories. That seems hard. But a not-completely-ridiculous approximation is that if a group of variables are all pairwise correlated at a level of ρ&gt;0, then the total information might be reduced by a fraction of ρ.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-8">[8]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>So how correlated are those features? In the social sciences, a correlation of 0.5 is considered quite high. That's plausible for some pairs of variables, e.g. age vs. health or political leaning vs. religious affiliation. But many of those correlations are are probably quite weak, e.g. age vs. native language or region vs. sex vs. marital status.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-9">[9]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Overall, my guess is that correlations reduce the total information by at least 10% but I doubt they reduce it by more than 60%. So I'd think the total information in the above features (if you could guess the categories perfectly) is somewhere between 10.6 and 23.9 bits. Let's take the average and call it 17.2 bits.</span></p><h2><span>Personality bits</span></h2><p><span>What about personality features? Let's use the same same recipe we used for demographic features, but faster: To start, let's give each of the 24 personality features five bins, in deference to </span><a href="https://dynomight.net/in-defense-of-myers-briggs.html#:~:text=Behold"><span>dynomight personality notation</span></a><span>. That would correspond to 24 × 2.32 = 55.68 bits total, because 2ᴷ ≈ 5 when K = 2.32.</span></p><p><span>Then we need to discount for correlations. The six main HEXACO personality factors are designed to be uncorrelated, but the different "facets" inside each factor are correlated (usually with a coefficient between 0.3 and 0.6). It seems reasonable to use an overall discount factor of 0.3 to reflect strong intra-factor correlations but weak inter-factor correlations. That suggests 39.0 bits overall.</span></p><h2><span>Style bits</span></h2><p><span>And what about writing style features? How much information do they contain?</span></p><p><span>This seems hard. Some of the features, like character n-grams are actually themselves long lists of features. (Frequency of typing </span><code><span>aaa</span></code><span>, frequency of typing </span><code><span>aab</span></code><span>, etc.) However, many of those features contain little information, since almost everyone types </span><code><span>zqx</span></code><span> around 0% of the time. And, of course, writing style features are correlated, since people who write </span><code><span>realise</span></code><span> instead of </span><code><span>realize</span></code><span> are less likely to put spaces around their em-dashes.</span></p><p><span>In absence of a better idea, I'm going to give one bit for each leaf node in the above list of style features. I think of this as giving each feature two bins, and then assuming that uneven distributions of features and correlations (which reduce information) are canceled out by the fact that many features deserve more than one bin and that there are probably more "deep" features that aren't listed (which increase information). This gives us the suspiciously round number of 50.0 bits.</span></p><h2><span>Guessing bits</span></h2><p><span>If you believe the above numbers, then we have at least 17.2 + 39.0 + 50.0 = 106.2 bits of identifying information that we leave clues about when we write. That's a lot. If you could see all those features, it would be enough to identify people even on a planet with 93 million trillion trillion people.</span></p><p><span>But to argue that the pseudpocalypse is nigh, it's not enough to argue that those bits exist. We need to argue that they </span><i><span>can</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>will</span></i><span> be guessed from a </span><i><span>relatively small</span></i><span> amount of text.</span></p><p><span>So obviously we need to talk about nuclear weapons. In a nuclear detonation, many unstable atoms are created. These spontaneously decay into more-stable atoms, in the process emitting radiation. Some types of atoms are very eager to decay, meaning they release a lot of radiation but stop existing within a few weeks (iodine-131). Others are reluctant to decay, meaning they don't release as much radiation but they stick around for decades (strontium-90). Others stick around for millions of years, but they produce so little radiation that they're not a big problem (cesium-135).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-10"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-10">[10]</a></sup></span><span>So, the residual radiation produced after a nuclear detonation is the sum of many different exponential curves, one for each isotope created during the detonation.</span></p><p><span>I suspect that identifying bits in text are sort of like that. Your level of formality and your average sentence length are revealed almost immediately. Your preference for latinate vs. germanic words takes a while to come through. And your social boldness and the fact that you live in Queensland rather than Southeast Australia are revealed very slowly, perhaps so slowly that it's effectively not revealed at all.</span></p><p><span>Right. So if you start with 106.2 bits, how many of those do you reveal after writing a given number of words?</span></p><p><span>I will answer that question through the noble method of making up numbers. But first, let's calibrate. You just read 4500 words written by me. How well could you guess my demographic and personality features? As a sanity check, I gave the above words to an LLM and asked it to guess. It did unnervingly well. It wasn't always right, but it usually was, and it did a great job of rating the confidence of the individual predictions.</span></p><p><span>I don't think there's any magical explanation for this. The fact is, if you look at the individual personality and demographic features, guessing them just isn't that hard. So I'm sure you could do just as well. And given enough time, I'm pretty sure you'd do even better for writing style features.</span></p><p><span>Even so, you're probably bad at it. Take the example of </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GeoGuessr"><span>GeoGuessr</span></a><span>, where people guess a location in the world from a random photo. Random people are sort of OK, but if you pick the top natural talents and have them practice obsessively, they're </span><i><span>really</span></i><span> good. I don't think LLMs are particularly good at guessing features from text, either. They weren't trained for it. It's just an emergent property of their general intelligence. The information-theoretic limit is surely much higher.</span></p><p><span>So here's a very rough cut: After 4500 words, I'd think it's possible to guess around:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>60% of the demographic features</span></li><li value="2"><span>70% of the personality features</span></li><li value="3"><span>80% writing style features</span></li></ul><p><span>If we model each of those with a separate exponential, and start them at 17.2 / 39.0 / 50.0 bits, then the total number of identifying bits that remain hidden after writing a given number of words is as plotted here:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-11"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-11">[11]</a></sup></span></p><img alt="decay_plot_10k.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784820126/lexical_client_uploads/neec65jqugokyps42zeq.svg" /><p><i><span>Et voilà</span></i><span>, pseudonymity is compromised when you leak 29 bits, which happens after 1071 words.</span></p><h2><span>Seriously?</span></h2><p><span>Of course not. The above figure stands on a creaking tower of tenuous assumptions. I've gone through the details of deriving that curve not because you should trust it, but because I think seeing the calculations makes the following points hard to argue with:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>You have far more than 29 bits of identifying information that you leak into your writing.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Some of those bits take a long time to get revealed, but others are revealed pretty quickly.</span></li><li value="3"><span>There are enough "fast leaking bits" that you can be identified from a writing sample that's "pretty small".</span></li></ul><p><span>I've made lots of debatable choices in terms of choosing features, assigning numbers of categories, estimating distributions across those categories, discounting for correlations, and guessing how many bins can be guessed. Those choices are all individually suspect. But the above points are supported by a pretty wide margin of error. You can make different choices, but it seems very hard to avoid concluding that the above three points are true.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-12"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-12">[12]</a></sup></span></p><h2><span>How would this work?</span></h2><p><span>You might be wondering why I'm using so many made-up numbers. After all, there's a whole field devoted to identifying authors from text, usually called </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stylometry"><span>"stylometry"</span></a><span> or "authorship attribution". They have research papers and </span><a href="https://pan.webis.de/"><span>competitions</span></a><span> and all that. However, as best I can tell, state of the art </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1109/ACCESS.2024.3407673"><span>published results</span></a><span> look something like this:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Take 50 people.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Get a few hundred writing samples from each author, each 1000-2000 words long.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Now, take a new writing sample from one of those authors.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Do some standard machine learning stuff.</span></li><li value="5"><span>Hey look, the author can be identified with ~95% accuracy!</span></li></ol><p><span>That sounds OK, but that's only identifying people against a pool of ~50 authors. For my claim to be true, similar accuracy would have to be possible with 490 million people. That's seven orders of magnitude more.</span></p><p><span>The thing is, the methods those papers are using are extremely weak. All the above math assumes that you're operating at the "information-theoretic limit", making perfect use of </span><i><span>all</span></i><span> available information. If you want to get close to that, we now have some idea how to do it: You apply the "modern" machine learning recipe of gigantic dataset + gigantic neural network + gigantic fortune spent on GPUs. My guess is that for us, that would require something on the order of "all the words ever written" + "tens of billions of parameters" + "tens of millions of dollars". I couldn't find a single paper that came remotely close to attempting that.</span></p><p><span>So I don't think those papers tell us much, for the same reason that a 3rd-order Markov model trained on a few books doesn't tell us much about how good computers could be at writing text. LLMs have shown that if you use the above recipe, then computers can get close to the information-theoretic limit for generating text.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-13"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-13">[13]</a></sup></span><span>So, I suspect that an LLM-level effort could achieve the same thing for identifying authors.</span></p><p><span>You might also wonder: Why am I talking about this as some possible future technology? Isn't that technology just LLMs?</span></p><p><span>I suspect the technology will be quite LLM-like in how it models human language. But current general-purpose LLMs aren't trained for this task. They're good at it "by accident". So, just like specialized chess AIs can crush LLMs at chess, I suspect specialized stylometry methods could crush general-purpose LLMs at stylometry. It's just that those specialized stylometry methods don't seem to exist yet, or at least aren't public.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-14"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-14">[14]</a></sup></span><span>So we shouldn't imagine that current LLMs are anything close to what's possible, even if you assume that generic LLM progress stopped today.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-15"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-15">[15]</a></sup></span></p><h2><span>Countermeasures</span></h2><p><span>If this is all true, what could be done about it?</span></p><p><span>The most obvious "countermeasure" would be to get used to it. I mean, imagine that we </span><i><span>did</span></i><span> live in a world in which everyone literally had to sign everything they wrote with a unique immutable string. What would happen? I'd expect a mixture of:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>People become more comfortable with their "full selves" being public, with less compartmentalization.</span></li><li value="2"><span>People pull back from communicating in public channels, relying more on group chats and the like.</span></li><li value="3"><span>People self-censor.</span></li></ol><p><span>There are strong historical analogies here, since over the past 20 years many governments and tech companies have in fact decreed that people must sign the things they write with their real names.</span></p><p><span>The effects seem to vary quite a lot based on the ambient culture and political system. Overall, my impression is that people are already much more comfortable with the idea that their work colleagues might read their dating profile or learn that they go to furry conventions. I'm optimistic that culture will continue to adapt to respect the fact that we all encompass multitudes. This seems healthy.</span></p><p><span>Some effects seem clearly positive. Self-censoring is not necessarily bad. For example, on the margin, real-names surely stop some teenagers from engaging in cyber-bullying. On the other hand, were you ever a teenager? I'm pretty sure that for anyone who is "different", having those differences broadcast to the world creates a much larger "bullying surface area". So the effects are mixed. And adults aren't as different from teenagers as we might like to think.</span></p><p><span>Twenty years ago, I might have predicted that real names would discourage people from expressing controversial political ideas online. Superficially, that seems completely wrong. At least in the West, lots of people are </span><i><span>very</span></i><span> happy to express minority political views, and if you disagree at all, then you can go to hell. But I also tend to think this hides a lot of self-censorship, where most people don't want engage in political mortal combat and so are cowed by a feisty minority. And, obviously, people in certain countries know that it's unwise to criticize the Party. So, getting used to it seems like an imperfect solution at best.</span></p><p><span>Another countermeasure would be to not build this technology, or not make it widely available. In the short term, this seems plausible. As far as I can tell, it's been possible for years for a modestly-funded group to build a phone app that would identify most people on the street from a photo. And yet, almost no one reading this has access to such an app. If general-purpose LLMs continue to get better at stylometry, it seems entirely possible that AI companies might decide it's a safety issue and train their AIs to refuse to do it.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-16"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-16">[16]</a></sup></span><span>This could work for a while.</span></p><p><span>But if the technology is possible, it seems certain that governments will build it and use it. They might try to keep it out of the hands of normal people. Certain governments might restrict their own use. My privacy-minded allies always seem very jaded, but it wouldn't surprise me at all if the Supreme Court declared that a warrant was needed before the FBI could de-pseudonymize a U.S. citizen. But when/if that technology becomes sufficiently cheap, it seems like it would be very difficult to keep it out of the hands of normal people and/or bad actors. My guess is that it's possible to create a program that's a few hundred gigabytes large and can run (slowly) on most modern laptops. If that program is made public, it would be hard to put the genie back in the bottle.</span></p><p><span>There are also technological countermeasures. Most obviously, you could run your writing through a "filter" to try to remove identifying bits, e.g. by asking an LLM to rewrite it. It's hard to be sure how well this would work, since we don't have accurate estimates of how many bits you're starting with or how many bits this would remove. But I'd guess this would be pretty effective if done carefully. The reason is that the number of identifying bits you leave in writing probably isn't </span><i><span>that</span></i><span> large, relative to the number needed to identify you. If you "homogenize" your writing to remove all style and personality, you should be able to remove most of those bits. Theoretically, you'll still leak some information. But I'd think this would substantially increase the amount you could write while remaining pseudonymous.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-17"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-17">[17]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>But after thinking about it, this makes me sad. Effectively, this countermeasure would preserve pseudonymity by taking writing and destroying all traces of humanity. It seems like this would work well for the "bad" uses of pseudonymity, like cyber-bullying or coordinated violence, but it wouldn't work at all for the "good" uses, like for example someone who likes to write pseudonymously because they feel like it allows them to be more honest and vulnerable and more fully themselves, damn it.</span></p><h2><span>Generalized pseudpocalypse</span></h2><p><span>Maybe this isn't just true for writing. Maybe it's just a feature of our universe that if you interact with the world in any significant way, then you leave traces that make it possible to identify you.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>If you walk around in public, then you can likely be identified by your face, your gait, your voice, your DNA, your retinas, or your literal fingerprints.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Or say you use the internet. Even if you lock down your browser fingerprint and hide your IP address using a VPN or Tor, a sufficiently powerful adversary could still identify you by analyzing global packet flow.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Or say you use </span><i><span>any</span></i><span> phone or computer. You might be identified through keystroke dynamics or the way you jiggle your finger or mouse.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Say you buy food at the grocery store, but you pay with cash and somehow shop at a grocery store with no cameras. If you buy more than a handful of items, I'd bet you can still be identified through the patterns in the stuff you buy.</span></li><li value="5"><span>(Incidentally, did you ever notice that cash has serial numbers on it? And did you know that more and more ATMs are starting to track those numbers?)</span></li><li value="6"><span>Or say you don't like your car being tracked, so you stop carrying a phone and somehow get lawmakers to outlaw license plates. Still, your car surely has a few small unique scratches, and the engine probably doesn't sound exactly the same as other cars, even from the same model and year. So if there's any high-resolution video or audio, that's still enough to track you.</span></li><li value="7"><span>Say you plug your headphones into a charging station at the airport. Your headphones have eccentricities in their analog charging circuits. If someone really wanted to, they could track that.</span></li><li value="8"><span>Or say you use electricity. Given high-resolution power-usage data, what can be said about how many people live with you? And what devices you're using? Probably a lot?</span></li><li value="9"><span>Or say you use a toilet. Many places already test sewage and know, at a population level, what drugs people are using and how prevalent various diseases are. Imagine this was upgraded to test many places in the system, with high temporal resolution, possibly correlated with flow measurements from individual houses. That would be exciting.</span></li><li value="10"><span>Or say you are a country and you have submarines. Can they be detected by adversaries using distributed acoustic sensing? What about satellite-based synthetic aperture radar? Gravity Gradiometers? Quantum magnetometry?</span></li></ul><p><span>As far as I can tell, the general trend is that without countermeasures, almost everything can be identified. Countermeasures can make it harder, but they're costly, and on the whole, the arms race seems to favor the identifier, not the person who doesn't want to be identified.</span></p><p><span>I stress: This is not all bad. The goodness / badness of a generalized pseudpocalypse depends on how society is structured. After all, the foundation of civilization is finding ways for people to make deals, and arguably less privacy makes that easier. The degree that we live in a </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vulnerable_world_hypothesis"><span>vulnerable world</span></a><span> where it's easy to create civilization-destroying technologies, perhaps we're very lucky to find ourselves in a non-private world. Still, I do worry that privacy has long provided a kind of "slack" from laws and norms. Historically, that slack has limited the power of institutions to enforce their rules. If privacy is going away, we need to think about how to preserve slack, particularly when institutions don't want to.</span></p><h2><span>Appendix: Section for skeptical information theory enthusiasts</span></h2><p><span>Above, I tried to estimate the number of bits of identifying information in writing. But what is a "bit"? In general, if </span><b><span>x</span></b><span> is a discrete random variable, then the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entropy_(information_theory)"><span>Shannon entropy</span></a><span> of </span><b><span>x</span></b><span> in bits is </span><b><span>H(x) = ∑ₓ p(x) log₂(1/p(x))</span></b><span>, where the sum is over all the values </span><b><span>x</span></b><span> can take. This is always bounded between zero and the logarithm of the number of values </span><b><span>x</span></b><span> can take.</span></p><p><span>That's fine, but "writing style" is not a discrete variable with a discrete number of categories. So how can I estimate the entropy of writing style? The short answer is that I can't. What I've actually estimated above is the </span><i><span>mutual information</span></i><span> between writing and writing style.</span></p><p><span>Let </span><b><span>s</span></b><span> be a random variable representing writing style. Think of this as some sort of high dimensional continuous vector representing all the quirks of how different people write. And let </span><b><span>x</span></b><span> be a writing sample of some length. This is discrete because we can represent writing on digital computers. Then what I've estimated above is the mutual information </span><b><span>I(x;s) = H(x) - H(x|s)</span></b><span>, where </span><b><span>H(x|s)</span></b><span> is the conditional entropy of </span><b><span>x</span></b><span> given </span><b><span>s</span></b><span>. This can be measured in bits because both </span><b><span>H(x)</span></b><span> and </span><b><span>H(x|s)</span></b><span> can be measured in bits. So that's what my estimate above really says: </span><b><span>I(x;s) ≈ 106.2 bits</span></b><span>.</span></p><p><span>Now, you still might be skeptical. Above, I've implicitly assumed something like the following was true:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>It's possible to identify one person out of N possibilities with low accuracy if and only if the mutual information between identifying features and writing is at least </span><b><span>log₂(N)</span></b><span> bits.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>That's how I justified pseudonymity being compromised around 29 bits. But is it really true? Strictly speaking, no. Actually, even more strictly speaking, it's "not even untrue" because it's not precise enough to be true or false. But as far as I can tell, basically any precise version of that statement is false. However, it's possible to find versions of that statement that </span><i><span>are</span></i><span> true, provided you add some extra not-too-crazy assumptions.</span></p><p><span>To start, let's consider an extremely simple model of information leakage.</span></p><p><b><span>Theorem.</span></b><span> Suppose the world consists of you plus </span><b><span>N</span></b><span> other people, and suppose each person has a binary identity string, drawn at uniform from the distribution over </span><b><span>M</span></b><span>-bit binary strings. All these strings are known to the attacker. Suppose you pick some subset of </span><b><span>K</span></b><span> bits and reveal them. Then the probability that this identifies you is</span></p><p><span>  </span><b><span>(1-2⁻ᴷ)ᴺ</span></b><span>.</span></p><p><span>Furthermore, in order to hold the probability of being identified below</span></p><p><span>  </span><b><span>(1-1/N)ᴺ ≈ exp(-1) ≈ 36.7%</span></b><span>,</span></p><p><span>it is necessary that </span><b><span>K ≤ log₂(N)</span></b><span>.</span></p><p><b><span>Proof.</span></b><span> The probability that all </span><b><span>K</span></b><span> observed features collide with any random person in the crowd is </span><b><span>2⁻ᴷ</span></b><span>. Thus, the probability of no collisions after checking the crowd of </span><b><span>N</span></b><span> people (meaning you are the only one matching the observed features) is </span><b><span>(1-2⁻ᴷ)ᴺ</span></b><span>. □</span></p><p><span>That's simple. But it's not realistic at all, since it assumes that people have immutable binary strings that they leak into their writing. Can we make it more realistic?</span></p><p><span>Well, there is a simple </span><i><span>lower</span></i><span> bound. That is, we can say in general that if the mutual information is significantly </span><i><span>less</span></i><span> than </span><b><span>log₂(N)</span></b><span>, then it's not possible to reliably identify someone.</span></p><p><b><span>Theorem.</span></b><span> Suppose N random people are selected and their full writing style features are made public. One person from that group is chosen and produces a writing sample. Then, the attacker must guess who produced it. The average success rate of the attacker (averaged over the random pool, the random choice of author, and the random writing sample) is at most </span><b><span>(I(x;s)+1)/log₂(N)</span></b><span>.</span></p><p><b><span>Proof.</span></b><span> Let </span><b><span>S=(s₁, s₂, s₃, …)</span></b><span> be the pool of </span><b><span>N</span></b><span> styles and let </span><b><span>n</span></b><span> be a random variable indicating which person was chosen. </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fano%27s_inequality"><span>Fano's inequality</span></a><span> says that the highest possible success rate is bounded by the conditional mutual information between the writing sample </span><b><span>x</span></b><span> and the identity </span><b><span>n</span></b><span>, conditioning on the pool of writing styles, i.e. the probability of success is at most</span></p><p><span>  </span><b><span>(I(x;n|S)+1)/log₂(N)</span></b><span>.</span></p><p><span>However, we can bound that conditional mutual information as</span></p><p><span>  </span><b><span>I(x;n|S) ≤ I(x;n,S) = I(x;n,sₙ) = I(x;sₙ) = I(x;s).</span></b></p><p><span>The first inequality is standard. The second step uses the fact that given </span><b><span>n</span></b><span>, the writing </span><b><span>x</span></b><span> is conditionally independent of all styles except the chosen writer. The third step uses the fact that </span><b><span>n</span></b><span> is conditionally independent of </span><b><span>x</span></b><span> given </span><b><span>sₙ</span></b><span>. The last step uses that </span><b><span>(x,sₙ)</span></b><span> is distributed as </span><b><span>(x,s)</span></b><span>. Substituting this bound gives the claimed result. □</span></p><p><span>So, if mutual information is much less than </span><b><span>log₂(N)</span></b><span>, reliable identification is impossible, even if the attacker knows all the style vectors perfectly. So, provided you don't leak that many bits, you're definitely safe.</span></p><p><span>But is the converse true? Does leaking more than </span><b><span>log₂(N)</span></b><span> bits always identify you? The general answer is </span><i><span>no</span></i><span>. The basic problem is that </span><b><span>I(x;s)</span></b><span> is the </span><i><span>average</span></i><span> information that an </span><i><span>average</span></i><span> person leaks in an </span><i><span>average</span></i><span> writing sample. Without further assumptions, you can construct scenarios where </span><i><span>some</span></i><span> rare people and writing samples contain gigantic amounts of information, but most people usually leak nothing. That would mean that the attacker is </span><i><span>very</span></i><span> certain in some cases but usually learns nothing.</span></p><p><span>So, to get a guarantee that identification is actually possible, you need to make some kind of additional assumption that the information leakage rate doesn't vary too much between different writers or between different things they write.</span></p><p><span>Suppose that </span><b><span>p(x,s)</span></b><span> is the joint distribution over writing styles </span><b><span>s</span></b><span> and writing samples </span><b><span>x</span></b><span>. Let's suppose that the attacker knows the true style vector </span><b><span>ŝ</span></b><span> for some person. Then, they will be given a writing sample </span><b><span>x</span></b><span> that either came from that person or came from a randomly chosen person, and must decide which. Formally, the attacker's goal is to guess if </span><b><span>x</span></b><span> was sampled from the writing distribution for that person, </span><b><span>p(x|ŝ)</span></b><span> or from the population marginal </span><b><span>p(x)</span></b><span>. Intuition suggests that the attacker's best strategy will be to look at the ratio</span></p><p><span>  </span><b><span>p(x|ŝ)/p(x),</span></b></p><p><span>and "accept" </span><b><span>x</span></b><span> as coming from </span><b><span>ŝ</span></b><span> if above some threshold, and reject it otherwise. In fact, the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neyman%E2%80%93Pearson_lemma"><span>Neyman-Pearson lemma</span></a><span> guarantees that this is the optimal strategy, in a very strong sense: That ratio contains </span><i><span>all</span></i><span> the information that's useful for making that decision.</span></p><p><span>Now here's something interesting: Instead of looking at the ratio, the attacker could look at the logarithm of the ratio. It makes no difference since it's monotonic. But if you take the logarithm of that ratio, and take the expectation over people and over texts, what do you get? Well:</span></p><p><span>  </span><b><span>𝔼 ln (p(x|s)/p(x)) = 𝔼 ln (p(x,s)/(p(x) p(s))) = I(x;s)</span></b></p><p><span>It's the mutual information! So, intuitively, the mutual information is how much an attacker learns about the style of the writer "on average", where that average is over both writers and text.</span></p><p><span>The following theorem will look at the average information in text for a writer with a particular style. I'll define this as</span></p><p><span>  </span><b><span>D(s) = KL(p(X|s) || p(X))</span></b><span>.</span></p><p><span>Intuitively, this is how different the writing of someone with style </span><b><span>s</span></b><span> is from the population average. That's because if you take the average of this value over different styles, you get the mutual information. That is, </span><b><span>I(x;s) = 𝔼[D(s)]</span></b><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-18"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-18">[18]</a></sup></span></p><p><b><span>Theorem (informal).</span></b><span> Suppose that the attacker will observe some text and wishes to classify it as either coming from a writer with specific known style </span><b><span>ŝ</span></b><span>, or coming from someone with a random style. Suppose that the attacker is only willing to tolerate some small risk </span><b><span>ε</span></b><span> of a false positive. Provided that </span><b><span>D(ŝ)</span></b><span> is significantly larger than </span><b><span>-ln(ε)</span></b><span>, the attacker can achieve that, while also keeping the risk of false negatives very low, provided that the variance of how much information is revealed in a random writing sample is bounded.</span></p><p><b><span>Theorem.</span></b><span> Let </span><b><span>D(ŝ) = KL(p(X|ŝ) || p(X))</span></b><span> to be the divergence between the target's writing distribution and the marginal distribution. Also, define </span><b><span>qₜ(x) ∝ p(x|ŝ)ᵗ p(x)¹⁻ᵗ</span></b><span> to be the family that interpolates between those two distributions. To formalize the idea that "information leakage" for </span><b><span>ŝ</span></b><span> doesn't vary that much, we assume that some constant </span><b><span>V</span></b><span> exists such that for </span><b><span>0 &lt; t &lt; 1</span></b><span>, the variance of </span><b><span>log(p(x|ŝ)/p(x))</span></b><span> under </span><b><span>qₜ</span></b><span> is bounded by </span><b><span>V</span></b><span>.</span></p><p><span>Then for any </span><b><span>ε</span></b><span> satisfying </span><b><span>exp(-D) &lt; ε &lt; exp(-D + ½ V)</span></b><span>, it is possible for the attacker to simultaneously achieve a false positive rate of </span><b><span>FPR ≤ ε</span></b><span> and a false negative rate of </span><b><span>FNR ≤ exp( - ½ (D+ ln ε)² / V).</span></b><span> This false positive rate reflects the mistake rate provided the writing sample </span><b><span>x</span></b><span> came from a randomly chosen other person, while the false negative rate reflects the mistake rate provided the writing sample </span><b><span>x</span></b><span> actually came from the person with style </span><b><span>ŝ</span></b><span>.</span></p><p><b><span>Proof sketch.</span></b><span> Let </span><b><span>f</span></b><span> be the distribution of </span><b><span>l(x) = log(p(x|ŝ)/p(x))</span></b><span> with respect to </span><b><span>p(x|ŝ)</span></b><span> and let </span><b><span>g</span></b><span> be the distribution of </span><b><span>l(x)</span></b><span> with respect to </span><b><span>p(x)</span></b><span>. The stated variance assumption implies a quadratic bound </span><b><span>K(u) ≤ D u +½ V u^2</span></b><span> for </span><b><span>-1 &lt; u &lt; 0</span></b><span>, where </span><b><span>K</span></b><span> is the cumulant generating function of </span><b><span>f</span></b><span>. Observe that </span><b><span>g</span></b><span> is an exponential tilting of </span><b><span>f</span></b><span>. The attacker's strategy must be to "accept" </span><b><span>x</span></b><span> as coming from </span><b><span>ŝ</span></b><span> if </span><b><span>l</span></b><span> is above some threshold </span><b><span>c</span></b><span> and "reject" it otherwise. Use </span><b><span>K</span></b><span> in a Chernoff bound on the probability </span><b><span>l</span></b><span> is less than </span><b><span>c</span></b><span> under </span><b><span>f</span></b><span> to upper-bound </span><b><span>FNR ≤ exp( - ½ (D-c)²/V)</span></b><span>. Now, using that </span><b><span>g(l) = exp(-l) f(l)</span></b><span>, again use </span><b><span>K</span></b><span> in a Chernoff bound on the probability </span><b><span>l</span></b><span> exceeds </span><b><span>c</span></b><span> under </span><b><span>g</span></b><span> to upper-bound </span><b><span>FPR ≤ exp( -c - ½ (D-c)²/V)</span></b><span>. Both of these bounds are simultaneously valid when </span><b><span>D-V &lt; c &lt; D</span></b><span>. Setting </span><b><span>c</span></b><span> to make the false-positive bound equal to </span><b><span>ε</span></b><span> gives </span><b><span>FPR ≤ ε</span></b><span> and </span><b><span>FNR ≤ exp( -½ (V - √(V² - 2V(D + ln ε)))²/V).</span></b><span> The latter can be relaxed into the stated result using that </span><b><span>√(1-x) ≤1-x/2</span></b><span> for </span><b><span>0 ≤ x ≤ 1</span></b><span>. □</span></p><p><span>Now, if we suppose that the attacker wants to find a particular person, with a particular known style </span><b><span>s</span></b><span>. And suppose that the attacker has a pool of </span><b><span>N</span></b><span> people and will see one writing sample from each, but wants to limit the total probability of a false positive to </span><b><span>δ</span></b><span> after seeing one sample from each person. Then, they will need that</span></p><p><span>  </span><b><span>(1-ε)ᴺ ≈ exp(-εN) = (1-δ),</span></b></p><p><span>which is satisfied by </span><b><span>ε ≈ δ/N</span></b><span>. Substituting this into the previous result says that the attacker can hold the </span><i><span>total</span></i><span> risk of a false positive to </span><b><span>δ</span></b><span> while achieving a false-negative risk of</span></p><p><span>  </span><b><span>FNR ≤ exp( - ½ (D(s) + ln δ - ln N)² / V).</span></b></p><p><span>These results use natural logarithms because the math is easier if you measure information in nats. If you measure information in bits then you would get </span><b><span>log₂ δ</span></b><span> and </span><b><span>log₂ N</span></b><span>. (Rescaling </span><b><span>D</span></b><span> and </span><b><span>V</span></b><span> appropriately.)</span></p><p><span>So, again, as long as the average information for user </span><b><span>s</span></b><span> is significantly larger than </span><b><span>log₂ N</span></b><span>, the attacker can identify that user with minimal risk of false positives.</span></p><p><span>Some writers might leak more information (higher </span><b><span>D(s)</span></b><span>) and some writers might leak less information (lower </span><b><span>D(s)</span></b><span>). But remember, </span><b><span>I(x;s)=𝔼 D(s)</span></b><span>. So as long as information leakage doesn't vary too much between people, and assuming that </span><b><span>I(x;s)</span></b><span> is much larger than </span><b><span>log₂ N</span></b><span> (and assuming that variance condition), almost everyone can be identified.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-1"><p><span>Editor's note: After this sentence was written, many additional hours were devoted to further idiotic tinkering. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-1"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-2"><p><span>It's fine. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-2"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-3"><p><span>A standard binary variable that is 0 or 1 with 50% probability conveys 1 bit of information, while a variable that is 0 / 1 / 2 with probability 49.8% / 49.8% / 0.4% conveys 1.0336 bits. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-3"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-4"><p><span>People born in certain decades are also presumably more likely to employ </span><i><span>see what I did there</span></i><span> gambits. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-4"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-5"><p><span>For example, here is the information content for seven different "bent coins":</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell coin)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.000</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.60</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.971</span></p></td></tr><tr><td id="fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-6"><p><span>Here's a more formal looking version of the table from the previous footnote:</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell class="table-cell"><p><span>0.722</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.90</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.10</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.081</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>You can generate that table by running this code:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><pre class="code-block"><span>from scipy.stats import entropy</span><br /><span> </span><br /><span>dists = ([0.5, 0.5], [0.6, 0.4], [0.7, 0.3], [0.8, 0.2], [0.9, 0.1], [0.95, 0.05], [0.99, 0.01])</span><br /><span>entropies = [entropy(p, base=2) for p in dists]</span><br /><br /><span>print("| p(A) | p(B) | Entropy |")</span><br /><span>print("|------|------|---------|")</span><br /><span>for i in range(len(dists)):</span><br /><span>    print(f"| {dists[i][0]:&lt;4.3f} | {dists[i][1]:&lt;4.3f} | {entropies[i]:&lt;7.3f} |")</span><br /></pre></pre><p><span>With three categories, the story is much the same. Things need to get quite uneven before information drops too much:</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>p(A)</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>p(B)</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>p(C)</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Entropy</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.333</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.333</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.500</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.250</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.250</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.500</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.600</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.200</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.800</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.100</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.100</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.922</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.900</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.050</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.025</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.025</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.336</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.990</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.005</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.005</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.091</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>You can generate that with this code:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><pre class="code-block"><span>from scipy.stats import entropy</span><br /><br /><span>dists = (</span><br /><span>    [1/3, 1/3, 1/3],</span><br /><span>    [.4, .3, .3],</span><br /><span>    [.5, .25, .25],</span><br /><span>    [.6, .2, .2],</span><br /><span>    [.7, .15, .15],</span><br /><span>    [.8, .1, .1],</span><br /><span>    [.9, .05, .05],</span><br /><span>    [.95, .025, .025],</span><br /><span>    [.99, .005, .005]</span><br /><span>)</span><br /><span>entropies = [entropy(p, base=2) for p in dists]</span><br /><br /><span>print("| p(A) | p(B) | p(C) | Entropy |")</span><br /><span>print("|------|------|------|---------|")</span><br /><span>for i in range(len(dists)):</span><br /><span>    print(f"| {dists[i][0]:&lt;4.3f} | {dists[i][1]:&lt;4.3f} | {dists[i][2]:&lt;4.3f} | {entropies[i]:&lt;7.3f} |")</span><br /></pre></pre><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-6"><span>↩︎</span></a></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-7"><p><span>Roughly speaking, we we should discount those maximum bits as follows:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Near even: No discount.</span></li><li value="2"><span>"Mildly uneven" (E.g. 70/30 with two categories) Discount by 10%.</span></li><li value="3"><span>"Quite uneven" (E.g. 90/10 with two categories) Discount by 50%.</span></li><li value="4"><span>"Extremely uneven" (E.g. 99/1 with two categories) Discount by 90%.</span></li></ul><p><span>The Shannon entropy of a categorical distribution is - Σᵢ pᵢ log₂ pᵢ. Or, in python:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><pre class="code-block"><span>import math</span><br /><span>def entropy(probs):</span><br /><span>	</span><span>return sum(-p * math.log2(p) for p in probs)</span><br /></pre></pre><p><span>Age: It's hard for me to imagine you could guess age from text with accuracy higher than 5 years. If you assume an age between 0 and 100, that would be 20 categories and log2(20)=4.32 bits. These are </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_the_United_States"><span>mildly non-uniform</span></a><span> so I'll reduce to 3.9.</span></p><p><span>Education: I'm assuming 6 categories: less than high school, high school, some college, finished college, master's degree, doctorate. That would be log2(6)=2.58 bits, but fairly uneven, so I'll reduce by 20% to reflect that.</span></p><p><span>Ethnicity: Assuming 62% white, 11% black, 16% latino, 6% asian, 1.5% indigenous, 3.5% mixed/other, and actually using the entropy formula.</span></p><p><span>Family status: I'm using two categories: Children / no children, on the logic that guessing the number of children would be very hard. These are mildly non-uniform, so I'll drop to 0.8 bits. You could have a third category for having children that are grown and that had left home, but this would be heavily redundant with age.</span></p><p><span>Income: The US census gives 11 income brackets. That seems as good a way of discretizing as anything. That would be log2(11) = 3.459 bits, but these are again moderately non-uniform, so I'll reduce to 2.5.</span></p><p><span>Marital status: I'm taking 3 categories (single, married, divorced / widowed / etc). That would be log2(3)=1.58 bits at maximum, but again these are somewhat non-uniform, so I dropped that to 1.2.</span></p><p><span>Mental health: I'm using 3 categories: "Healthy", "chronic condition", and "severe issues". Assuming 73% healthy 25% chronic condition, 2% "severe issues", and using the entropy formula gives 0.9 bits.</span></p><p><span>Native language: I'm using 2 categories, namely "English native", and "non-English native". These are pretty uneven inside the Anglosphere, so I'll drop from 1 bit to 0.6 bits.</span></p><p><span>Occupation. The BLS classification gives 23 major groups. That would be log2(23)=4.523 bits, but it's moderately non-uniform, so I'll reduce to 4 bits.</span></p><p><span>Physical health: Assuming 60% "healthy" 30% "chronic condition" 10% "severe issues" and using the entropy formula.</span></p><p><span>Political leanings: I'm using three categories (left, center, right). These are fairly uniform so I'm using 1.58 bits.</span></p><p><span>Region: I asked an LLM to divide the Anglosphere up into a number of regions with reasonable granularity. With some tinkering, it gave 23 regions: South East England, South West England, Midlands, Northern England, Scotland, Wales, Republic of Ireland, Northern Ireland, Quebec, Ontario, Western Canada, Atlantic Canada, Northeast US, Southern US, Midwest US, Western US, Alaska, Hawaii, Southeast Australia, Western Australia, Queensland, Central &amp; Southern Australia, New Zealand. With LLM-generated population estimates (which looked reasonable) and plugging into the entropy formula, this gave 3.5481 bits.</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>#</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Region</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Pop (M)</span></p></th><th These are uniform-ish.</span></p><p><span>Sex: 2 categories, near-even </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-7"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-8"><p><span>Consider a set of binary random variables, each of which is equally likely to be 0 and 1, yet all are correlated with a pairwise correlation coefficient of ρ. There are many distributions that satisfy this condition, but a natural choice is an Ising model. If there are many variables, then the entropy per-variable in an Ising model with pairwise correlations of ρ tends to h((1+√ρ)/2), where h is the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binary_entropy_function"><span>binary entropy function</span></a><span>. We can print out those numbers:</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>ρ</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>h((1+√ρ)/2)</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.0000</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.00000000</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.1000</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.92661216</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.2000</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.85048963</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.3000</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.77121926</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.4000</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.68826012</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.5000</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.60087604</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.6000</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.50801160</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.7000</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.40803633</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.8000</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.29811751</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.9000</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.17212786</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.0000</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.00000000</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>As you can see, the entropy per-variable is always a bit more than 1-ρ. But the Ising model is optimistic, in the sense that it has the highest entropy of all distributions meeting the given conditions. So, screw it, let's estimate the entropy per-variable to just be 1-ρ. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-8"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" class="table-cell"><p><span>-0.1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>-0.1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.0</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>-0.1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.0</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.0</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.3</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.0</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.0</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>Personally, this doesn't mean very much to me… </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-9"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-10"><p><span>It's more complicated than this, because some atoms (e.g. strontium-90) emit more energy per decay than others. And some types of radiation are more harmful to human life than others. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-10"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-11"><p><span>In general, if you want an exponential curve f(n) that starts at 1 for n=0 and decays to 1-X for n=N, you should choose f(n) = exp(n × ln(1-X) / N). So for demographic features we're using X=0.6 and N = 4500, meaning f(n) = exp(-0.00020362 × n). For personality features, we're using X=0.7, meaning f(n) = exp(-0.00026755 × n), and for writing style features, we're using X = 0.8, meaning f(n) = exp(-0.000357653 × n). So the total number of bits remaining hidden is 17.2 × exp(-0.00020362 × n) + 39.0 × exp(-0.00026755 × n) + 50.0 × exp(-0.000357653 × n). </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-11"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-12"><p><span>OK, what's the most likely reason I might be wrong? Above, I used math to estimate the information in features, and then I basically made up numbers for how much of that information can be guessed from text. Even so, my greatest concern is that the first part. I'm a bit worried that I might be overestimating the amount of information in the features themselves due to inadequately discounting for correlations. For one thing, there are probably correlations between feature groups. (For example, I'd bet that people who are high in perfectionism are less likely to use </span><i><span>lose</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>loose</span></i><span> interchangeably, and that people who live in Northern England are more likely to use the character string </span><code><span>colour</span></code><span> than people who live in Hawaii.) Also, my crude method of discounting information by ρ due to pairwise correlations of ρ might not discount enough: I used an estimate based on an Ising model, which is the maximum-entropy (highest information) distribution given the correlation constraints. I haven't been able to figure out how much lower the information could be in the worst-case. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-12"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-13"><p><span>People debate if this is true for "intelligence", but it's definitely true in terms of bit-rate. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-13"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-14"><p><span>Also, arguably, stylometry is about language. This means that large </span><i><span>language</span></i><span> models probably have much of what they need baked in. That might explain why they're pretty good at it just "by accident". But to do this optimally I think they'd need self-reflection (e.g. access to probabilities of text given different contexts) that current LLMs aren't typically capable of, and wouldn't know how to manipulate correctly without task-specific training. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-14"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-15"><p><span>You could conjecture that near-optimal stylometry abilities are some kind of "emergent property". But the general lesson so far is that LLMs mostly don't have emergent properties but are just good at what they're trained at. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-15"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-16"><p><span>(Meta-joke about you—person who works at an AI company—thinking, "maybe we should do that", coming to this footnote, and seeing this meta-joke.) </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-16"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-17"><p><span>Instead of "homogenizing" writing by imposing a generic style, perhaps it would be better to "camouflage" it by enforcing a very strong but random style. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-17"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-18"><p><span>Be a little careful here: Typically, the KL-divergence is understood to be measured in nats. But in this article, I've measured mutual information in bits. That's fine, but you need to convert. For example, 106.2 bits = 73.60 nats. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-rSqhrTukn3GZ8oJcE-18"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eaxoBCmQd4vew9ZXX/pseudpocalypse#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Lightcone Commons](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tjeoLz2GzfFMysZhg/lightcone-commons)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span>TLDR:</span></b><span> </span></p><blockquote><p><span>Lightcone Commons is a new funding platform for coordinating large-scale ambitious philanthropy. </span></p><p><span>We recruit thinkers with strong track records to make grant recommendations to funders. Anyone giving away $100k+ per year is welcome to join. We are facilitating ~$20M of grants in our first round, and hopefully more every 3 months after that.  Evaluators are paid 2% of recommendations, and we charge a 3% platform fee. </span></p><p><span>We handle all logistics and due diligence for funding recommendations to charities, individuals, and for-profits. Funders maintain full control over their funds, and there are no vetoes or constraints on what recommendations we generate.</span></p><p><span>Apply for funding </span><a href="https://www.lightconecommons.com/apply" rel="noreferrer"><span>here</span></a><span>.</span></p></blockquote><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784831559/lexical_client_uploads/gmjf9u8tizglqgpk0cqo.png" /><p><span>I am launching </span><a href="http://www.lightconecommons.com"><span>Lightcone Commons</span></a><span>, our software-first platform for distributing philanthropic funding. We connect funders with giving opportunities, coordinate splitting the bill with others who want to fund the same projects, and make it easy to defer to grant evaluators who vet applications and scout for new grantmaking opportunities. Funders can leave and join the platform at any time and without the need to commit any funds in advance.</span></p><p><span>I and my team (together with </span><a href="https://survivalandflourishing.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span>SFC</span></a><span>, Andrew Critch, and </span><a href="https://survivalandflourishing.fund/s-process" rel="noreferrer"><span>others</span></a><span>) have been developing software used to distribute over $100M in grants through SFF and Lightspeed Grants. This new platform brings that technology (and much more) to a broader audience.</span></p><p><span>Finding high-quality philanthropic opportunities is difficult. Potential grantees are incentivized to pitch themselves aggressively, often making evaluation adversarial. Many of the most effective grants are small. Information about grantees is scattered across the internet, and it is often very hard to tell how much room for funding an organization has. Successfully giving away many millions of dollars without falling prey to grifters, confidence games, or plain bureaucratic conformity, is a great challenge that the vast majority of philanthropists fail.</span></p><p><span>When faced with these problems, many funders ask family members or other trusted long-time friends to manage their giving. But misalignment between a philanthropist and their foundation is common, and philanthropic foundations rarely manage to attract world-class evaluator talent.</span></p><p><span>To solve these problems, we solicit thousands of applications from organizations and individuals aiming to do highly cost-effective work across a wide range of worldviews (usually with a focus on benefiting humanity as a whole, on a large scale), and we maintain a network of evaluators with strong track records to investigate giving opportunities (including potential grantees that didn’t apply).</span></p><p><span>We facilitate debates, arguments, and collaborations between evaluators to improve grant recommendations. Funders can watch and read those (both live and in recorded form), to help them become calibrated about the quality of evaluator recommendations and to help them decide how to delegate their funding decisions (if at all). Any participating funder is welcome to bring their own evaluators into the process.</span></p><p><span>We plan to organize a funding round every 3 months. As an interested funder you gain full access to all applications and almost all evaluations before you make any funding commitment. Our web platform lets you simulate how you and others would split the bill on any grants multiple people want to fund and where exactly your money would go if you decided to contribute to the grant portfolios of any participating evaluators.</span></p><h1><b><span>Funder FAQ</span></b></h1><p><b><span>When is the next funding round?</span></b></p><p><span>Applications for the first round open today! To be eligible for this grant round applications have to come in before August 22nd, and we will announce grant recommendations by October 22nd. From then on we aim for one funding round every 3 months with rolling applications (so the same application still works if you apply after the 22nd of August, you’ll just get a response 3 months later).</span></p><p><b><span>How much money do you plan to disburse?</span></b></p><p><span>For the first round, I am expecting participating funders to disburse around $15M–25M. Hopefully similar amounts every 3 months (and growing over time).</span></p><p><b><span>Who are the funders participating in the first round?</span></b></p><p><span>Funders can withdraw and join the round at any time, so this is in-flux, but our current tentative list of funding for the first round is:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>$10M from Jaan Tallinn (conditional on us getting $10M from other funders)</span></li><li value="2"><span>~$5M from Dustin Moskovitz for the first round (and $10M over the first year if the first round goes well)</span></li><li value="3"><span>$3M+ from an anonymous donor</span></li><li value="4"><span>$2M+ from a different anonymous donor</span></li><li value="5"><span>~$2M from the Long Term Future Fund</span></li><li value="6"><span>~$2M from the ARM Fund</span></li><li value="7"><span>~$200k from Timothy Telleen-Lawton</span></li><li value="8"><span>~$100k from Andreas Stuhlmüller</span></li></ul><p><b><span>How do I sign up to participate in this or the next grant round as a funder?</span></b></p><p><span>Please book an onboarding call with me right </span><a href="https://calendly.com/oliver-habryka/meeting"><span>here</span></a><span>. While we place no strict requirements on who can access the platform, I want to make sure we are on the same page about treating applicant information as appropriately sensitive, and that you assign some substantial probability to distributing at least $50,000 in the coming round.</span></p><p><b><span>Do you take any fees?</span></b></p><p><span>Evaluators receive 2% of any funding directed through them, and we additionally charge a 3% platform fee (for a total of 5% on top of the grant amounts). We lower platform and evaluator fees for funders who distribute large amounts of money through the platform.</span></p><p><b><span>How does this whole process work at a technical level?</span></b></p><p><span>The funding process works on two simple principles:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>You fund what you value the most</span></li><li value="2"><span>When several people want to fund the same thing, they split its cost in proportion to how much money they are giving away</span></li></ol><p><span>There are two main classes of participants in the Lightcone Commons: funders and evaluators.</span></p><p><span>All funders and evaluators enter a </span><b><span>funding order, i.e. the ideal order</span></b><span> in which they would distribute money in the round. For instance:</span></p><p><span>"Project Alpha is most effective, so I want to fund that until marginal money given to it is as good as funding project Beta, at which point I want to fund them both until they are as good as project Gamma, etc. until eventually I run out of money".</span></p><p><span>Evaluators do so for projects, and funders have the extra option of doing the same for evaluators. For instance:</span></p><p><span>"Alice seems to have the best judgement, so I want to fund her until her recommendations are only as good as evaluator Bob’s recommendations, at which point I want to fund them both until their recommendations are only as good as Carol’s, etc."</span></p><p><span>When two funders want to fund the same evaluator, they split the bill in proportion to their total budgets. When two evaluators/funders want to fund the same organization, they too split the bill in proportion to their total budgets. If one funder wants to fund a project for up to $100k, and the other one wants to fund it to $150k, then they split the bill on the first $100k, and the second funder who is more excited about the project and wants to fund it more, covers the remaining $50k.</span></p><p><span>A virtue of this system is that it tells us immediately how to reallocate funds if anyone's funding order changes, or a funder's total budget changes, while following a simple rule for ensuring that costs get split fairly between participating funders.</span></p><p><span>If you want to build a more intuitive understanding, you can play around with this widget:</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><br /></p><p><b><span>How much time and effort should a funder expect to put into a grant round?</span></b></p><p><span>The key thing I recommend funders spend their time on is to figure out which evaluators they trust to produce good grant recommendations, and when the recommendations by an evaluator hit diminishing marginal returns. If you brought your own evaluator to a grant round, then you could make a final funding decision in as little as 2-3 hours of getting yourself familiarized with the process, the UI, plus your onboarding call.</span></p><p><span>If you do not have a strong pre-established trust relationship with any evaluators in the round, I recommend that you budget on the order of 10 hours to read evaluations and meeting notes or watch recordings of debates and discussions between the evaluators, and to read other content written by the evaluators that helps you understand their worldviews.</span></p><p><span>You can also participate by funding organizations directly, without deferring to any evaluators. In that case, I recommend more like 50 hours to have enough time to read through applications, ask organizations questions, and make a proper grantmaking portfolio.</span></p><p><span>But ultimately, if you end up finding that you don't have the time to come to a sufficiently robust assessment of any evaluator or organization to make you think giving to them is a good idea, you can just decide to reduce your giving. You are not committing to any time investment by signing up as a funder.</span></p><p><b><span>Who are the evaluators currently providing grantmaking portfolios on the platform?</span></b></p><p><span>We actively recruit people with expertise and competence to evaluate grants on our platform. Some evaluators currently confirmed are:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Zvi Mowshowitz, author of widely read AI-analysis Substack "Don't Worry About the Vase"</span></li><li value="2"><span>Yafah Edelman, chief strategy officer at </span><a href="https://epoch.ai/about/team/yafah-edelman"><span>Epoch AI</span></a></li><li value="3"><span>Eliezer Yudkowsky, co-founder of MIRI and early AI alignment researcher</span></li><li value="4"><span>Nate Soares, president of the Machine Intelligence Research Institute</span></li><li value="5"><span>Caleb Parikh, managing director of the AI Risk Mitigation Fund and head of the Long Term Future Fund</span></li><li value="6"><span>Elizabeth Van Nostrand, widely read author of </span><a href="http://acesounderglass.com"><span>AcesoUnderGlass.com</span></a></li><li value="7"><span>Me! (Oliver Habryka, CEO of Lightcone Infrastructure)</span></li><li value="8"><span>Katja Grace, lead researcher at AI Impacts, is confirmed to join for future rounds</span></li></ul><p><span>And I am actively working on getting more evaluators on the platform, with examples of who I would love to have on board being:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Scott Alexander, widely known author of SlateStarCodex and AstralCodexTen</span></li><li value="2"><span>Ryan Greenblatt, Chief Scientist at Redwood Research</span></li><li value="3"><span>Ajeya Cotra, former Program Director in AI Safety at Coefficient Giving (formerly known as Open Philanthropy)</span></li><li value="4"><span>Ben Hoskin, managing director of VARA, a highly successful hedge-fund, and author of a number of very high quality historical AI charity reviews</span></li><li value="5"><span>Eric Neyman, former researcher at ARC, now focused on identifying high-leverage giving opportunities in policy and advocacy</span></li><li value="6"><span>Richard Ngo, former policy researcher at OpenAI, and widely read author on AI safety and AI policy</span></li><li value="7"><span>Kelsey Piper, staff writer at </span><a href="https://www.theargumentmag.com/"><span>The Argument</span></a><span> and author of many great essays on altruism, ambition, and philanthropy</span></li><li value="8"><span>Daniel Kokotajlo, head of AI Futures Project, author of AI 2027 and ex-OpenAI governance researcher</span></li><li value="9"><span>Gavin Leech, co-founder of </span><a href="https://arbresearch.com/"><span>Arb</span></a><span> and co-author of </span><a href="https://press.stripe.com/scaling"><span>The Scaling Era</span></a><span> with Dwarkesh Patel and author of </span><a href="https://www.goodreads.com/author/show/52864507.Gavin_Leech"><span>1,188 book reviews</span></a></li><li value="10"><span>Rohin Shah, lead of the AGI Safety &amp; Alignment team at Google Deepmind</span></li></ul><p><span>(Just to avoid any ambiguity, the people in this second list have not agreed to participate as evaluators! They have not endorsed this process! But it seemed useful to give a realistic sampling of people I am aiming to get on board.)</span></p><p><b><span>Do you only generate grant recommendations to recognized 501(c)3 organizations?</span></b></p><p><span>No. We do not require applicants to have 501(c)3 status, and coordinate grants and investments to for-profits, individuals, educational institutions, 501(c)4 organizations, non-US organizations and individuals. Funders can specify arbitrary restrictions on what kind of organizations or individuals they want to fund, depending on what makes sense from their ethical, legal or tax perspective.</span></p><p><b><span>Do evaluators only evaluate grants to organizations that apply via the Lightcone Commons application process?</span></b></p><p><span>No. Any evaluator can recommend grants to any organization (or private individual) that they think is a good funding target. We only ask the evaluator to fill out basic information about the organization, such as what kind of legal entity they are, and to perform some basic due diligence to avoid fraud.</span></p><p><span>We have also imported recent live applications from the Long Term Future Fund, </span><a href="http://manifund.org"><span>Manifund.org</span></a><span> and </span><a href="http://grantmaking.ai"><span>grantmaking.ai</span></a><span>, and we are hoping to share more applications with other funding bodies in the future, to make life easier for applicants.</span></p><p><b><span>I run a philanthropic regranting program/foundation/fund. Does it make sense for me to participate in Lightcone Commons?</span></b></p><p><span>Yes! Many of the funders participating in our first round are funds or foundations, not just individuals (such as the </span><a href="https://funds.effectivealtruism.org/funds/far-future"><span>Long Term Future Fund</span></a><span> and the </span><a href="https://www.airiskfund.com/"><span>ARM Fund</span></a><span>).</span></p><p><span>You can have your staff join as evaluators, and use your existing decision-making processes to decide your preferred funding order. You can import any existing grant proposals you are evaluating into the round, which means you get to potentially split the bill with other funders on those grants. You also get access to other institution’s funding applications, increasing your pool of potential funding targets, and get to read and learn from the grant evaluation practices of other evaluators and institutions.</span></p><p><b><span>What happens at the end of a grant round?</span></b></p><p><span>When a grant round gets finalized, every funder is asked to lock in their final amount of funding and allocation order. At that point each funder can see what their final grant recommendations will be. When they are locked in, the funder commits to us and the other participating funders and evaluators that they will send out the funds for these grants in the next 2 months. If they fail to do so, we bar them from ever participating in any Lightcone Commons round again (and will try our best to find replacement funders for the affected organizations).</span></p><p><b><span>How do you coordinate multiple funders each wanting to review and change their final grant allocation?</span></b></p><p><span>When any funder makes a change to their final budgets and allocations, all other funders get a chance to do the same in response. Only if everyone explicitly approves their final grant allocation do we move forward with the end of the round. If this somehow ends up going in cycles, I will coordinate some kind of compromise, or make an executive decision to remove a funder from the round (though this seems to me very unlikely to occur).</span></p><p><b><span>Do I need to facilitate sending out the money myself? That sounds like a lot of work.</span></b></p><p><span>No. For charitable donations, we have a nonprofit that can receive your donation and handle sending out the grants, final due diligence, and logistics. For grants to non-501(c)3 entities, we have an LLC that creates and signs contracts with target organizations.
