The costs for these services are covered by the 3% platform fee on all recommendations (which to be clear is charged regardless of whether you make use of these services).</span></p><p><span>Both of these are optional and you can also make whatever donations or grants directly from your own DAF, LLC or private bank account (though this does not reduce the platform fee).</span></p><p><b><span>Do you ever block grants to any organizations or individuals?</span></b></p><p><span>We pose no restrictions on organizations that evaluators want to recommend grants to, and provide no mechanism for evaluators or funders to block grants by other funders. I aim for Lightcone Commons to be a platform that facilitates coordination between funders, evaluators and organizations with a wide variety of worldviews, many of which will strongly disagree with each other. I think the world will be a lot better if we can nevertheless successfully coordinate, critique each other's plans, and split the bill whenever many people want to see the same thing funded, even if they diverge strongly on other issues.</span></p><p><span>(If you wish to use the nonprofit and LLC grantmaking services, I will do my best to make whatever grants you want to make, but do maintain the option to refuse to make any specific grants, and will occasionally face legal restrictions on the grants we can make)</span></p><p><b><span>What if a project needs funding sooner than 3 months from now?</span></b></p><p><span>In addition to facilitating funding rounds every 3 months, we also make it easy for funders to run a “direct grantor program” where invited grantors get a special budget that allows them to make grants immediately and unilaterally. This enables more active grantmaking (where the grantors are able to take the time to talk with and get to know a grantee), and allows grantors to use this unilateral grant budget to insure projects against the risk of not getting funding in the next grant round (which enables more risk-taking).</span></p><p><span>When a grantor funds a project like this, let’s say for $10,000, then in the next grant round the first $10,000 allocated to that project will, instead of going to the grantee, be used to reimburse the grant budget of the grantor.</span></p><p><span>Beyond that, funders are encouraged to increase the grantor budget of those who made grants that ended up being evaluated as highly impactful, and partially reimburse the budget of those who made grants that almost made the cut of the grant round. We provide UI on our platform to make this easy.</span></p><p><span>Funders can specify what level of review they want such unilateral grants to undergo before they go out. If they use our nonprofit and LLC grantmaking services we can accommodate both a manual review by the funder, a simple checklist process run by our staff, or a full unilateral system where grants go out as soon as possible.</span></p><p><b><span>How do I create a direct grantor program?</span></b></p><p><span>You can invite anyone you want as a direct grantor onto the platform in the “Direct Grants” section of the app.</span></p><p><span>Any grantor who participates in another funder’s direct grants program can also choose to be listed as available for being a grantor for other funders. One of the easiest ways to get started with a direct grantor program is to look through the existing list of direct grantors, and give the ones you think will make good grants by your lights a direct granting budget.</span></p><p><b><span>I am a speculation grantor at the Survival and Flourishing Fund (SFF). Can I use my budget to make unilateral grants to Lightcone Commons grantees?</span></b></p><p><span>There will probably be some allowance for this; see the Grantor Program FAQ addressed at SFF speculation grantors </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1GUs4_wSaaU8y8IWo5qMSmZoc6mDgpgD45fqssrlDHvc/edit?tab=t.i9zimhaiyoq"><span>here</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><b><span>Are all evaluations shared with everyone?</span></b></p><p><span>It is up to the evaluators to decide who to share their evaluations with. The platform encourages evaluators to make their evaluations visible and transparent to all participating funders and other evaluators, which makes it more likely that funders will decide to defer to their grantmaking portfolio, and more likely that other evaluators will engage critically with their evaluations. But we provide tools to make evaluations only visible to subsets of funders and other evaluators, and if some evaluations involve sensitive information, or we grow to have a large enough number of participating funders such that expectations of privacy become an issue, I expect more evaluators to limit their evaluations to a narrower audience.</span></p><p><b><span>Are the evaluators compensated for their work?</span></b></p><p><span>Yes. Evaluators receive the 2% fee charged on any grants given based on their recommendations.</span></p><p><span>Sometimes a funder wants to bring in an evaluator who is already on their payroll in some fashion; in that case it’s between the evaluator and the funder to determine what to do with the 2% evaluator fee (which should plausibly go to whatever organization employs the evaluator instead of the evaluator directly, or should just be waived for the case of the funder directing money through their own employed evaluator).</span></p><h1><br /></h1><h1><b><span>Applicant FAQ</span></b></h1><p><b><span>What kinds of projects do you expect to fund?</span></b></p><p><span>That’s not up to us! Since any participating funders maintain full discretion about where their funding goes, there is no super short and simple description of what projects will get funding. That said, I am happy to say a few things about what the funders who are currently participating in the platform have historically funded and have publicly said about their giving priorities.</span></p><p><span>One of the biggest funders in the upcoming round is </span><b><span>Jaan Tallinn</span></b><span> who has conveniently </span><a href="https://jaan.info/philanthropy/"><span>written publicly</span></a><span> about his philanthropic priorities. He has also been the primary funder behind the Survival and Flourishing Fund, which has made </span><a href="https://survivalandflourishing.fund/recommendations"><span>all of its grant recommendations over the last 6+ years public</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>At a high level, I would summarize Jaan’s giving as “a very intellectually diverse approach to trying to shape the long-term future of humanity”. His giving has historically involved recruiting people he respects with quite different perspectives on the world, and giving them lots of latitude on what they can recommend funding to.</span></p><p><span>This has produced grants ranging from </span><a href="https://www.earthlawcenter.org/"><span>“advocacy to give legal rights to rivers”</span></a><span> to </span><a href="https://vitalismfoundation.org/"><span>“longevity research</span></a><span>” to funding for </span><a href="http://pronatalist.org"><span>pronatalist.org</span></a><span>, and AI x-risk reduction work at </span><a href="https://palisaderesearch.org/"><span>Palisade Research</span></a><span>. The bulk of his funding goes to work aimed at aligning, controlling or otherwise shaping superintelligent AI systems, but the tail of his giving is very diverse and it’s hard to rule out much ambitious charity.</span></p><p><span>One of the other big funders for the first round is </span><b><span>Dustin Moskovitz</span></b><span>. </span><a href="https://medium.com/@moskov/works-in-progress-the-long-journey-to-doing-good-better-9dfb68e50868"><span>His most recent essay</span></a><span> about his giving is probably most helpful for understanding his perspective. Coefficient Giving (previously known as Open Philanthropy) has also facilitated over a billion dollars of grants of his, many of which are publicly available on their </span><a href="https://coefficientgiving.org/funds/"><span>fund pages</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>The </span><a href="https://funds.effectivealtruism.org/funds/far-future"><b><span>Long Term Future Fund</span></b></a><span> and the </span><a href="https://www.airiskfund.com/"><b><span>ARM Fund</span></b></a><span> are both contributing ~$2M each for this round. They both focus almost exclusively on how we can shape the development of AI in a positive direction, but are also both open to infrastructure-level interventions to achieve that aim, such as by funding Effective Altruist, Rationality or AI-safety community building, supporting U.S. voting reform, and occasionally the LTFF might also make a grant to avoid other existential risks, like engineered pandemics.</span></p><p><span>The rest of the funding comes from smaller donors or anonymous donors whose giving I know little about! My guess is they will also skew pretty focused on AI existential risk topics, but I really have a lot of uncertainty here.</span></p><p><b><span>Do I need to be a nonprofit to apply?</span></b></p><p><span>No! We can make grants to 501c3s, individuals, and for-profit organizations. For individuals, we will determine whether your work qualifies as charitable, and provide you with fiscal sponsorship if so. Some of our funders can only make charitable grants (but others aren't limited to charitable grants, so you will be considered in either case).</span></p><p><b><span>Do I need to be in the US to apply?</span></b></p><p><span>No! We accept applications from anyone internationally. However, there are some countries where it is in my experience approximately impossible to send money to (in particular Russia and India), so it’s pretty likely we can’t make a grant to you if you are located in one of those places.</span></p><p><b><span>What's the deadline for applications?</span></b></p><p><span>We accept rolling applications, with a deadline two months in advance of when grants are recommended. The current round closes on </span><b><span>August 22, 2026</span></b><span>, for grant recommendations made by (roughly) </span><b><span>October 22, 2026</span></b><span>. Any applications after that will get a response by (roughly) January 22, 2027.</span></p><p><b><span>What does a typical application process look like?</span></b></p><p><span>We try hard to make the application process quick and straightforward. The application has only four required sections:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>What are you working on?</span></li><li value="2"><span>What would you do with funding?</span></li><li value="3"><span>Who is involved?</span></li><li value="4"><span>Logistical details about your project</span></li></ol><p><span>You can structure your answers to the first three questions however you want, and the application interface is a live-editable collaborative document.</span></p><p><span>We might also already have an application from you, if you applied to the </span><a href="https://funds.effectivealtruism.org/funds/far-future"><span>Long Term Future Fund</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://manifund.org/"><span>Manifund</span></a><span>, or </span><a href="http://grantmaking.ai"><span>grantmaking.ai</span></a><span> in the past few months. If so, you should have received an email with instructions on how to get edit access to your imported applications.</span></p><p><span>You can update and edit your application any time after you submit it, so don’t worry about getting it all perfect immediately. If your plans have changed, we encourage you to revise your proposal accordingly.</span></p><p><span>After the application deadline, evaluators and funders using the platform will consider your proposal and make recommendations for how much funding they think it should receive. You can read more about our evaluators and how recommendations work in [section]. Any applications received after the deadline will be evaluated in the next round, three months later.</span></p><p><span>Most projects will hear back when we announce recommendations, usually two months after the application deadline (October 22nd for the first round). You should receive money within a month after that (and technically you could fail due diligence as we try to send you money, though I expect that to happen very rarely, and it can take longer if you are located outside of the US).</span></p><p><span>Some projects may hear back shortly after submitting, if your project appeals to a direct grantor (individuals who were given a budget by a funder to unilaterally make grants). Direct grants will be limited for the first round, but our goal is that in future rounds most successful applicants will receive at least partial funding within 1-2 weeks of applying.</span></p><p><b><span>Can I submit multiple applications?</span></b></p><p><span>You can submit multiple applications for your organization, but please try to avoid them being mutually exclusive or overlapping too much. We might consolidate applications ourselves on the backend if you submit multiple, and leave it up to you how to allocate funding between your projects if you do get funding.</span></p><p><br /></p><h1><b><span>Evaluator FAQ</span></b></h1><p><b><span>What's the time commitment for being an evaluator?</span></b></p><p><span>You'll likely need at least 40-50 hours of up-front work when you first start evaluating, plus 20–30 hours each round (quarterly) to update your recommendations. This is a lower bound! Many evaluators will spend much more time than that.</span></p><p><span>The up-front work consists of getting familiar with the applicant pool and writing about your taste and strategy, to help funders decide whether they want to defer to you. After that, you'll have new applications to read each round, but you'll be making updates to an existing set of priorities, not starting fresh.</span></p><p><b><span>Will I be compensated for evaluation work?</span></b></p><p><span>Yes, evaluators are paid 2% of any funding directed through them.</span></p><p><b><span>What does evaluation work involve?</span></b></p><p><span>In short: read applications, and rank them in a priority order for funding.</span></p><p><span>You'll produce three kinds of outputs:</span></p><p><i><span>1. Your funding priority order (most important)</span></i></p><p><span>Your funding order is the answer to the question “in what priority order would I recommend distributing funding if I was the only funder in this round”.</span></p><p><span>This kind of ranking allows your priorities to be compared directly to other evaluators', and used to allocate a variable amount of funds. Funders defer to evaluators by placing you in their own funding order, so the amount flowing through your recommendations is not known in advance and shifts as funders join, leave, and adjust their allocations. For the details of our algorithm see “How does this whole process work on a technical level?”.</span></p><p><span>Your funding order can and should be considerably longer than the amount funders allocate to you, given the way collective funding is allocated. When several evaluators want to recommend the same organization, they split the bill in proportion to the budget they're working with, so your recommendation tends to go further than expected.</span></p><p><i><span>2. Explanation of your funding priorities</span></i></p><p><span>You are strongly encouraged, but not required, to explain your funding order. Explanations will frequently consist of writeups about specific organizations for other funders and evaluators to read, based on each organization's application. But high-level, general material is also useful – your views on cause prioritization, what you look for as signs of strong teams or projects, and your predictions about the future will all help funders understand the thinking behind your recommendation, and feel more confident in deferring to you.</span></p><p><i><span>3. Engagement with other evaluators</span></i></p><p><span>You are strongly encouraged to engage with other evaluators – reading and commenting on their explanations, answering questions on your own, and debating points of disagreement. Applications and evaluations live in documents that support top-level and inline comments, with configurable visibility, and the platform includes features for notifications and direct messages to help evaluators manage many parallel threads. We also encourage recording and posting synchronous debates, that other funders and evaluators can watch.</span></p><p><b><span>Can evaluators be anonymous or pseudonymous?</span></b></p><p><span>Technically, yes. You can sign up with either your real name or a pseudonym, which others on the platform will use to track your priorities and commentary across applications and rounds.</span></p><p><span>I expect pseudonymity to be most useful when specific funders bring in evaluators who wish to remain anonymous. Currently, the only other way to become an evaluator is for me (Oliver Habryka) to personally select you to participate, which is less likely for evaluators who don't want their identity revealed. Pseudonymous evaluators are also less likely to be given additional funding to allocate by new funders, since it makes it difficult to find other information about them outside the platform.</span></p><p><b><span>Who can see an evaluator's presence, funding priorities, explanations, and comments?</span></b></p><p><span>By default, your identity on the platform will be visible to all other evaluators and funders – and we aren't asking anyone to commit to secrecy about which evaluators are part of the program.</span></p><p><span>Your funding priorities, explanations, and discussions have a few different options for visibility. By default, your funding order will be visible to all funders and evaluators on the platform. Other documents you write –&nbsp;including explanations of your reasoning, and evaluations of specific organizations/applications – can have more granular visibility settings. Options for those documents are:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Private, visible only to you</span></li><li value="2"><span>Visible only to a specific set of people you've shared it with</span></li><li value="3"><span>Visible to all funders and evaluators, but not to </span><a href="https://www.lightconecommons.com/#what-if-a-project-needs-funding-sooner-than-3-months-from"><span>direct grantors</span></a><span> or observers</span></li><li value="4"><span>Visible to everyone on the platform besides applicants: funders, evaluators, grantors, and observers</span></li></ul><p><span>Your comments on other evaluators' work will be visible to everyone who can see the document you commented on – usually everyone on the platform, but sometimes only the specific set of people they've shared it with.</span></p><p><b><span>What tools are available for screening and evaluating applications?</span></b></p><p><span>All applications are read by a screening team as soon as they come in, so by the time you read an application, it will be tagged with:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Multiple initial 1–5 star ratings from the Lightcone Commons staff</span></li><li value="2"><span>A Pangram score</span></li><li value="3"><span>Tags related to cause area and problem domain</span></li></ul><p><span>In future rounds you'll also be able to see past comments about organizations that have applied, from evaluators who made their comments broadly visible.</span></p><p><span>We give all evaluators a generous LLM token budget and a set of AI tools we've developed to help evaluate applicants. We don't require you to use a specific rubric for evaluation, so you can build whatever workflow works for you.</span></p><p><span>You also don't need to read every application. Your funding priorities will be more useful to funders in proportion to how many applications they cover, but you're free to stick to your areas of expertise and ignore applications outside of it.</span></p><p><b><span>Can an organization be an evaluator?</span></b></p><p><span>Yes! A fund, foundation, or research group can participate as an evaluator, and receive the 2% evaluator compensation as an organization.</span></p><p><b><span>I've been an evaluator for SFF. How is the Lightcone Commons process similar and different?</span></b></p><p><span>The basic process is the same: evaluators submit funding priorities and comment on other evaluators' priorities, and projects are funded in proportion to recommended budgets.</span></p><p><span>The biggest change is </span><b><span>ongoing engagement</span></b><span>: participation in Lightcone Commons is intended as an ongoing engagement, as opposed to the round-based engagement in SFF rounds. Evaluators are welcome to only participate for a single round and then become inactive, but they are also welcome to keep submitting evaluations as an ongoing long-term activity.</span></p><p><span>The other differences are:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>No required meetings.</span></b><span> You can technically participate without ever talking to another evaluator. But discussion with other evaluators helps funders calibrate on how closely your priorities match theirs, so evaluators who don't engage in discussion will likely see less money routed through them.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>No requirement to review all applications.</span></b><span> You can make recommendations that only cover the applications you feel confident in evaluating, though your funding priorities will be more useful to funders if they include more applications.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>No speculation-grant filter.</span></b><span> Grants no longer need to have received a speculation grant to be considered in a round.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>Matching pledges get no special treatment.</span></b><span> Applicants can mention them; you should treat them as ordinary evidence about cost-effectiveness.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span>No special tracks or sub-area rounds</span></b></li></ul><p><br /></p><h1><b><span>Misc FAQ</span></b></h1><p><b><span>What are the biggest changes from the way the Survival and Flourishing Fund has been organizing their funding rounds?</span></b></p><p><span>I aim to reach almost complete feature parity with the SFF S-Process, but am planning to substantially redesign a few non-core parts of the process where I am dissatisfied with the current implementation.</span></p><p><span>First, I want to remove special code and special treatment around matching pledges. Grantees are welcome to indicate in their application that they are seeking matching pledge funding, but evaluators should evaluate whether those matching pledges increase the cost-effectiveness of a grant the same way they evaluate all other evidence about the cost-effectiveness of a grant.</span></p><p><span>Second, I intend to remove requirements for every grant to first receive a speculation grant in order to be evaluated in a grant round on the same basis as other applicants. Furthermore, I want to remove any requirement by evaluators to look comprehensively at all applications submitted to a grant round. Evaluators are encouraged to build grant portfolios that limit themselves to domains where they have expertise.</span></p><p><span>Third, I expect to rebuild the tools for getting AI systems to assist with evaluations from the ground up, mostly just because I don’t like the current rubric-based system and want to give evaluators more freedom on how to leverage AI systems in evaluating grants.</span></p><p><span>Fourth, I want to remove requirements for evaluators to attend meetings and engage with other evaluators in highly specific ways. It is OK for an evaluator to be on the platform and never talk to anyone else (though doing so will likely result in funders trusting them less and distributing less funding through them). I want to create affordances and incentives for engaging with the evaluations of other funders both live and asynchronously, but not make it a requirement.</span></p><p><span>Fifth, I am not planning to have any kind of special tracks, or special grant rounds focused on sub-areas, which feel more opinionated than makes sense for neutral infrastructure like this (though individual funders and congregations of funders are welcome to do special promotions or requests for proposals, and we’ll likely help at least with the basic logistics of those).</span></p><p><b><span>Is the code for this going to be open source?</span></b></p><p><span>Eventually yes, but I am not guaranteeing that we will have made it open source by the first grant round. A project being open source introduces a few more security risks (such as accidentally leaking credentials or tokens in the code) that I would like to not have to think about early on, but I am aiming to make it possible for someone to fork the code behind this project and run their own copy of it within a few months of getting started.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tjeoLz2GzfFMysZhg/lightcone-commons#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Challenge: Hand coding weights for efficient sequence memorisation](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KWtchKwwnJkd4bwCi/challenge-hand-coding-weights-for-efficient-sequence-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>We hand coded weights for one layer MLPs that memorises labels for input token sequences of length two. The number of facts our hand-coded models can memorise with 90% accuracy</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-1">[1]</a></sup></span><span>scales roughly linearly with the models' parameter count</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-2">[2]</a></sup></span><span>, just like trained models for the same architecture. However, our hand-coded models' scaling prefactor still falls short of trained models' by a factor of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. A hybrid solution in which we handcode the MLP input weights and learn the MLP output weights, which amounts to a linear classification problem</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-3">[3]</a></sup></span><span>, falls short by a factor of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="hero_figure_alt.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784829313/lexical_client_uploads/puejio3anrtwyicmiifo.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 0:</span></b><span> Max number of facts each model can learn when requiring 90% accuracy on evaluation. The fitted lines are best fit for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> solving for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span>We pose a challenge to the community:</span></b><span> Find better constructions than ours that can memorise more facts in the same number of weights and close the gap to the trained solution further.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-4">[4]</a></sup></span></p><h1><span>Background: Why sequence memorization, and why an MLP?</span></h1><p><span>The goal of this research is to get a clearer understanding of how look-ups are encoded in LLMs.</span></p><p><span>We expect that a lot of the information stored in the weights of an LLM is memorized facts, rather than general circuits. We don't assume a clean separation between what is a "general circuit" vs a "memorized fact", but a clear example of the former is this </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01148"><span>addition circuit</span></a><span>, and a clear example of the latter is </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/hpWHhjvjn67LJ4xXX/p/iGuwZTHWb6DFY3sKB"><span>knowing what sport some specific athlete is playing</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>The goal of mech-interp is to be able to take a model (possibly together with its training data), and pick it apart into different components that do human-understandable tasks. Since we expect that many of these tasks are factual look-ups, it would be useful to know what we should expect look-up to look like in a transformer model.</span></p><p><span>Prior work already points at the MLP layers as the main storage site: </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2012.14913"><span>Geva et al.</span></a><span> describe transformer feed-forward layers as key-value memories, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2104.08696"><span>Dai et al.</span></a><span> identify individual MLP neurons that control specific facts, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2202.05262"><span>ROME</span></a><span> locates and edits factual associations in mid-layer MLPs, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.07229"><span>MEMIT</span></a><span> inserts thousands of facts into mid-layer MLPs with closed-form weight updates, and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.05405"><span>Allen-Zhu &amp; Li</span></a><span> measure how many bits of knowledge a transformer can store per parameter. On the toy-model side, a recent theory line models transformer weight matrices as associative memories that store facts as sums of outer products: </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.00802"><span>Bietti et al.</span></a><span> for bigram lookups, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.02984"><span>Cabannes et al.</span></a><span> for the capacity of linear memories over random embeddings (only </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> facts), and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.06538"><span>Nichani et al.</span></a><span>, who prove that one-layer transformers can store a number of facts linear in their parameter count, up to log factors.</span></p><p><span>None of this yet amounts to a weight-level account of </span><i><span>how</span></i><span> the storage in real models actually works. That's the level of understanding we're after.</span></p><p><span>In this post, we study sequence memorization as a toy model for any lookup where some combination of input signals carries a meaning that is substantially different from a linear combination of the individual signals.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-5">[5]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Note that the kinds of sequences we consider here are deliberately structureless. Random input pairs are mapped to random labels, so there is nothing to compress. The model can do nothing except memorize.</span></p><p><span>We mostly focused for now on creating algorithms that memorise as much as possible, not yet on trying to understand how exactly the solutions trained models use work. The hope is that once we have any solution at all that performs somewhat similarly to the trained models', figuring out what exactly the trained models are doing might become a lot easier. That does mean that we focus on algorithms that qualitatively seem to us like they're not too far removed from something gradient descent methods might be able to learn.</span></p><h1><span>Outline</span></h1><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Training data</span></b><br /><span>In this section we describe the training data for the sequence memorization task: The model sees two input tokens and must predict one output label. The input→output mappings ("facts") are generated uniformly at random, so the model can only memorize them.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Testing Various Model Architectures</span></b><br /><span>We take a one layer transformer, make every architectural component optional (attention, MLP, norms, residual connections, biases), train every variant, and measure the maximum number of facts each one can learn. Main findings: the MLP is by far the most important component, and replacing attention with a simple sum of per-position token embeddings works as well or better — in this task, attention seems to do nothing beyond linearly mixing the two tokens' information into one position.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Scaling</span></b><br /><span>We scale up two variants: the full one layer transformer, and a heavily stripped-down variant (embeddings + ReLU neurons + unembedding). Both store a similar number of facts, scaling as roughly </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> with model dimension, close to what we'd expect from a first principles information theory perspective.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>Challenge: Benchmark for understanding</span></b><br /><span>Can you write down weights for the toy model by hand, or with any algorithm that isn't gradient descent, such that it matches the performance of a trained model? Being able to do this is a benchmark for how well we understand how the model stores facts. In this section we lay out the rules and evaluation criteria, and invite you to give it a try.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span>Our attempt</span></b><br /><span>Our</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-6">[6]</a></sup></span><span>attempt to solve the above challenge. When only 90% accuracy is required, our construction matches the trained models' scaling exponent, at roughly 9.7× fewer facts. At 100% accuracy it falls further behind as models grow. If we hand-code only the embedding matrix, learning the unembedding matrix with gradient descent amounts to a linear classification task. This boosts the number of facts we learn to roughly 3.5× fewer than the fully trained model, at both 90% and 100% accuracy thresholds. Non-zero progress, but much room for improvement.</span></li></ul><h1><span>Training data</span></h1><p><span>The training data are sequences of three tokens, two input tokens and one output token. Given an input of two tokens, the network is trained to predict the next token (i.e. the output token).</span></p><p><span>The code for generating the training data is not very long, and is quoted below in full, if you prefer to read code.</span></p><p><span>Hyperparameters for the data generation:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><code><span>n_facts</span></code><span> -- Number of facts.</span></li><li value="2"><code><span>input_vocab_size</span></code></li><li value="3"><code><span>output_vocab_size</span></code></li><li value="4"><code><span>seed</span></code><span> -- Random seed. </span><i><span>This value is always 42 in our experiments.</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-7">[7]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span>The code first generates a list of every possible input combination. Then this list is shuffled, and the first </span><code><span>n_facts</span></code><span> pairs from the shuffled list are used as the inputs for the </span><code><span>n_facts</span></code><span> facts. These facts are then divided as equally as possible among the </span><code><span>output_vocab_size</span></code><span> target labels.</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span>Code</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><pre class="code-block"><span>def generate_facts(n_facts: int, # of facts to generate, </span><br /><span>                   input_vocab_size: int, # of unique tokens in the vocabulary</span><br /><span>                   output_vocab_size: int, # of unique targets</span><br /><span>                   seed: int = 42</span><br /><span>                  ) -&gt; dict[str, torch.Tensor]:</span><br /><span>    </span><br /><span>    if n_facts &gt; input_vocab_size ** 2:</span><br /><span>        raise ValueError(f"Cannot generate {n_facts} unique facts with a vocabulary of size {input_vocab_size}. Maximum unique facts: {input_vocab_size ** 2}")</span><br /><span>    </span><br /><span>    device = torch.tensor(0).device  # respect default device</span><br /><span>    generator = torch.Generator(device=device).manual_seed(seed)</span><br /><br /><span>    all_possible_inputs = torch.cartesian_prod(torch.arange(input_vocab_size),</span><br /><span>                                               torch.arange(input_vocab_size))</span><br /><br /><span>    inputs = all_possible_inputs[torch.randperm(all_possible_inputs.size(0),</span><br /><span>                                                generator=generator)[:n_facts]]</span><br /><span>    </span><br /><span>    targets = torch.arange(n_facts) % output_vocab_size</span><br /><span>    sorted_indices = torch.argsort(targets)    </span><br /><br /><span>    return {"inputs": inputs[sorted_indices], </span><br /><span>            "targets": targets[sorted_indices]}</span></pre></div></details><h1><span>Testing Various Model Architectures</span></h1><p><span>We want to find out how these facts can be encoded in a one layer transformer. However, that turned out to be hard. But if we know in what part of the model the main action is, then maybe we can simplify the toy model to only that part and start with understanding that.</span></p><p><span>To test what parts of the model are important for the sequence memorization task, we made a transformer model, where every part of the model can be turned on or off. Then we trained all variants of this model and compared their performance.</span></p><p><span>The full toy model consists of:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Token embeddings</span></li><li value="2"><span>Positional embeddings</span></li><li value="3"><span>A single full width attention head</span></li><li value="4"><span>An MLP layer (on the last token position only since we're not trying to predict intermediate tokens)</span></li><li value="5"><span>Two residual connections, one past the attention, and one past the MLP.</span></li><li value="6"><span>Token unembedding to create the logits for the target tokens.</span></li><li value="7"><span>Three RMS Norms, one applied to the input to the attention, one to the input to the MLP and one to the input to the unembedding.</span></li></ul><figure class="image"><img alt="Memory Toy Model - Full.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784833271/lexical_client_uploads/gwlfha5iwznbqn100qii.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 1</span></b><span>: The full toy transformer model, with all the different parts present.</span></p><p><br /></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>After the attention, the model only continues its computation in the second token position. This is because the model is only trying to predict the third token, and not the second token.</span></p><h2><span>Model variations</span></h2><h3><span>Mixing</span></h3><p><span>We want to be able to simplify the model by removing the attention. However, the problem with doing so is that the information from the first token has to reach the second token position somehow. Therefore, we can't just remove the attention, but will have to replace it with something else.</span></p><p><i><span>Mixing</span></i><span> is the part of the model that combines the first token and second token information into the same residual stream. We have three different variants for this.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Learned Attention (Lrn Attn):</span></b><span> Standard transformer attention.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Uniform Attention (Unif Attn):</span></b><span> Same as above except we remove the attention pattern</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and replace it with a uniform </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-8">[8]</a></sup></span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Dual Embedding (2Emb):</span></b><span> There is no attention and no positional embedding. Instead, there are two different token embeddings, one for each position. These are simply added together to make the initial residual stream activation.</span></li></ul><h3><span>MLP</span></h3><p><span>There are a number of variants regarding the MLP. Firstly the MLP can either be present or be missing. Secondly if there is an MLP layer, each of the following can be varied</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Activation Function (Act)</span></b><span> can be either GELU or ReLU.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Bias</span></b><span> can be included or not.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-9">[9]</a></sup></span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Residual connection (Res)</span></b><span> around the MLP can exist or not.</span></li></ul><h3><span>Norms</span></h3><p><span>The norms can also be turned on and off. Each of the norms for the read-in to the attention and MLP only exists if both that part of the network is present (Unif Attn or Lrn Attn for the attention), and Norms are turned on. The last norm, just before the unembedding only depends on the norm setting, and is there if norms are turned on and not there if norms are turned off.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Memory Toy Model - Simple.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784833434/lexical_client_uploads/dl35lpyy2lmxydkysjxp.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 2: </span></b><span>A simplified version of the toy model. The MLP is present but everything else (attention, norms, residual connection around the MLP) is turned off.</span></p><p><br /></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span>Results</span></h2><p><span>The following is just a summary of the results. To see the full results, including the details of the experimental setup, see Appendix B.</span></p><p><span>To find out which parts of the network matter for the memorization task, we trained every combination of the architectural variants described above, and measured the maximum number of facts each one could learn. All models in this experiment used the same size:</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-10"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-10">[10]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>The networks are trained using gradient descent</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-11"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-11">[11]</a></sup></span><span>, on a cross entropy loss. We say that they have successfully learned some number of facts if, at the end of training, taking argmax over the logits always gives the correct label. To find the maximum learnable facts for a specific architecture variant, we do a binary search over the number of facts.</span></p><p><span>Note that this is measuring the maximum number of facts </span><i><span>that Adam can find weights for</span></i><span>, not how many facts an architecture can represent in theory. So, some of the observed effects below (definitely the learned-attention results, possibly also the norm results) are likely about trainability rather than architectural capacity.</span></p><p><span>In the bullets below, percentage ranges like "X% - Y% more facts" give the range of the effect of one setting across all combinations of the other settings. For absolute numbers see table B.1 in Appendix B.</span></p><p><span>The </span><b><span>MLP</span></b><span> is by far the most important part.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Adding the MLP block lets the network learn </span><b><span>60% - 373%</span></b><span> more facts, a much larger effect than any other setting.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The effect is </span><i><b><span>largest</span></b></i><span> when </span><b><span>Mixing</span></b><span> = </span><b><span>2Emb</span></b><span> or </span><b><span>Unif Attn</span></b><span>, combined with </span><b><span>Norms</span></b><span>=❌, I.e, when the MLP's ReLU or GELU neurons are the only non-linearity in the network.</span></li></ul><p><b><span>Mixing</span></b><span> is the second or third most influential setting.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-12"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-12">[12]</a></sup></span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>For most settings </span><b><span>2Emb</span></b><span> </span><i><span>beats</span></i><span> </span><b><span>Unif Attn</span></b><span> (</span><b><span>7.8% - 39%</span></b><span> more facts), which </span><i><span>beats</span></i><span> </span><b><span>Lrn Attn</span></b><span> (</span><b><span>5% - 39%</span></b><span> more facts)</span></li><li value="2"><span>Except when </span><b><span>MLP</span></b><span>=❌ and </span><b><span>Norms</span></b><span>=❌ (nothing else in the network besides the mixing and the unembedding), then </span><i><span>all the mixing options do equally well</span></i><span>.</span></li></ul><p><span>Uniform attention being better than learned attention has to be due to learned attention having training difficulties, since learned attention is strictly more expressive. Consistent with this, the learned attention results are also by far the least stable across repeated runs. It's not surprising that dual embedding does better than uniform attention, since it's both strictly more expressive</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-13"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-13">[13]</a></sup></span><span>and should be no harder to train.</span></p><p><span>These results suggest that the attention probably isn't doing anything importantly different from just linearly adding together the embeddings of the two tokens.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-14"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-14">[14]</a></sup></span></p><p><b><span>Norms</span></b><span> is the second or third most influential setting.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-12"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-12">[12]</a></sup></span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>When </span><b><span>MLP</span></b><span>=✅ then adding norms lets the network learn </span><b><span>2.2% - 42%</span></b><span> more facts</span></li><li value="2"><span>When </span><b><span>MLP</span></b><span>=❌ then adding norms lets the network learn </span><b><span>79% - 174%</span></b><span> more facts</span></li></ul><p><b><span>The remaining settings</span></b><span> matter less.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Res</span></b><span>=✅ is mostly better than </span><b><span>Res</span></b><span>=❌, with </span><b><span>-0.7%</span></b><span> to </span><b><span>34%</span></b><span> more facts</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Bias</span></b><span>=✅ is mostly better than </span><b><span>Bias</span></b><span>=❌, with </span><b><span>-5.1%</span></b><span> to </span><b><span>22%</span></b><span> more facts</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>GELU</span></b><span> is mostly better than </span><b><span>ReLU</span></b><span>, with </span><b><span>-0.2%</span></b><span> to </span><b><span>13%</span></b><span> more facts</span></li></ul><p><span>Finally, there is one notable outlier: the combination </span><b><span>MLP</span></b><span>=✅, </span><b><span>Norms</span></b><span>=✅, </span><b><span>Res</span></b><span>=❌, </span><b><span>Bias</span></b><span>=❌, </span><b><span>ReLU</span></b><span> (combined with </span><b><span>any Mixing</span></b><span> option) does far worse than the individual settings would predict, almost as bad as having no MLP at all.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-15"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-15">[15]</a></sup></span><span>We don't know why.</span></p><h1><span>Scaling</span></h1><p><span>How does the number of learnable facts grow with model size? To find this out, we picked two of the very many model architecture varieties and scaled them up.</span></p><p><span>These models are:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><i><b><span>Full:</span></b></i><span> See Figure 1</span><br /><b><span>Mixing</span></b><span>=</span><i><b><span>Lrn Attn</span></b></i><span>, </span><b><span>MLP</span></b><span>=✅, </span><b><span>Norms</span></b><span>=✅, </span><b><span>Res</span></b><span>=✅, </span><b><span>Bias</span></b><span>=✅, </span><b><span>Act</span></b><span>=</span><b><span>GELU</span></b></li><li value="2"><i><b><span>Simple:</span></b></i><span> See Figure 2</span><br /><b><span>Mixing</span></b><span>=</span><i><b><span>2Emb</span></b></i><span>, </span><b><span>MLP</span></b><span>=✅, </span><b><span>Norm</span></b><span>=❌, </span><b><span>Res</span></b><span>=❌, </span><b><span>Bias</span></b><span>=❌, </span><b><span>Act</span></b><span>=</span><b><span>ReLU</span></b></li></ul><p><span>We test how many facts each of these models can learn for a range of model dimensions </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> with</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span class="math-tex"></span><span>,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-10"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-10">[10]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span class="math-tex"></span><span>,</span></li><li value="3"><span class="math-tex"></span><span>,</span></li><li value="4"><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></li></ul><p><span>And the result is</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="68747470733a2f2f6861636b6d642e696f2f5f75706c6f6164732f726b616c4975695647782e706e67.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784833533/lexical_client_uploads/lsf5dbmlfjpvhnafstov.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 3: </span></b><span>Maximum number of facts a network can learn vs model dimension.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>The number of facts each model can learn scales roughly as </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. This is basically what we'd expect a priori from an information theory perspective, because the number of bits in the model's parameters scales with</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. The number of bits required to store a label scales with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><h1><span>Challenge: Benchmark for understanding</span></h1><p><span>Can we write down weights for the sequence memorization toy model, either by hand or with some algorithm that isn't raw numeric optimisation à la gradient descent, such that the resulting model matches the performance of a trained model?</span></p><p><span>Ultimately we also want this algorithm to yield weights and neuron activations qualitatively similar to those real models produce, but for now we're mostly focusing on raw performance.</span></p><p><span>There are two reasons why this is a useful framing.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>If we understand how the facts are embedded, we should be able to replicate this, without gradient descent.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Thinking about "How would I do this?" can be a useful framing for figuring out what some trained model is doing.</span></li></ul><p><span>We think that our current best attempt (which is presented further down) is some non-zero progress on this challenge, but there is still far to go. We encourage all readers to give it a try.</span></p><h2><span>Rules</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><span>Use the model architecture described below. </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-16"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-16">[16]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span>Use the code in section "Training data" to generate facts.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Come up with an algorithm that generates the model weights, if given a list of facts to encode.</span></li><li value="4"><span>You can't produce the weights with gradient descent, or with any other generic black-box optimizer.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-17"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-17">[17]</a></sup></span><span>Closed-form computations, greedy algorithms, and combinatorial constructions are all fine. For example, a ridge regression would be allowed. The spirit of the rule is that the algorithm should embody an explanation of </span><i><span>how</span></i><span> the facts get stored, not merely find weights that happen to work.</span></li><li value="5"><span>You can do a hyperparameter sweep over hyperparameters in your algorithm.</span></li><li value="6"><span>Evaluate your models using the evaluation criteria below.</span></li></ul><h2><span>Model architecture</span></h2><p><span>Most of the sequence memorization capacity is in the MLP. We therefore propose focusing on a toy model with only this part and everything else cut out. This would be something like the </span><i><span>simple</span></i><span> model in the previous scaling experiment (Figure 2). However, that architecture still has unnecessarily many weights, a legacy of being a cut-down version of the full model in Figure 1: the MLP's input matrix sits directly after the embeddings, and its output matrix directly before the unembedding. Two consecutive linear maps can always be folded into one, so we absorb</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> into the two embedding matrices and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>into the unembedding, with no loss of expressivity. Doing so gives us this architecture:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Memory Toy Model - Hand Coded.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784833558/lexical_client_uploads/qoyjfuhrplk1lp9ynedi.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 4: </span></b><span>This model architecture is equivalent to the toy model configuration with settings Mixing=2Emb, MLP=✅, Norms=❌, Res=❌, Bias=❌, Act=ReLU.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Use this architecture for the challenge if you want to be able to compare your results with ours. However, if you want to go for something slightly different, or even the full 1-layer transformer, we'd still be interested in what you can do.</span></p><p><span>Note that in our model architecture, </span><b><span>the MLP's hidden dimension </span></b><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is always scaled to stay smaller than the input vocabulary </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>**, and this seems somewhat load-bearing. For the case </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, we already have a more efficient construction which we think probably gets pretty close to the theoretical capacity limit, though it doesn't seem like something a model is likely to learn in training. See Appendix A for that construction.</span></p><h2><span>Evaluation Criteria</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Max facts, acc=1</span></b><br /><span>What is the maximum number of facts you can give the model such that argmax of the output logits gives the correct labels for every fact.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Max facts, acc &gt;= 0.9</span></b><br /><span>Same as above except argmax of the output logits only needs to give the correct labels on 90% of the facts.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Hybrid</span></b><br /><span>Same evaluation criterion as either of the above. However, your algorithm only has to generate the embedding matrix; the unembedding is trained.</span></li></ul><p><span>For each of the four criteria,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-18"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-18">[18]</a></sup></span><span>see how the maximum number of facts scales with model size.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Can you get the same scaling exponent as fully trained models?</span></li><li value="2"><span>Can you get the same pre-factor (or close to) as the fully trained models?</span></li><li value="3"><span>Can you do better than us on either of the above?</span></li></ul><h2><span>How this challenge relates to known constructions</span></h2><p><span>Hand-coded memorization has a long history in learning theory: </span><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/0885064X88900209"><span>Baum (1988)</span></a><span> memorizes </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> binary-labeled points in </span><i><span>general position</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-19"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-19">[19]</a></sup></span><span>in</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>hidden threshold units, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2006.02855"><span>Bubeck et al.</span></a><span> give a ReLU version of Baum's construction with near-optimal weight magnitudes, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1810.07770"><span>Yun et al.</span></a><span> prove a one-hidden-layer ReLU net can memorize a general-position</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-class dataset if its width is at least </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.03187"><span>Vardi et al.</span></a><span> memorize </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> points with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> parameters, though only via depth </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> networks whose weights each also need to carry </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> bits. None of these results transfer here: they need at least one and often multiple of neuron count greater than </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, multiple hidden layers, or inputs that are in general position</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-19"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-19">[19]</a></sup></span><span>, which token sequences are not.</span></p><p><span>Just before publishing this, we actually found one construction in the literature that seems promising: </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.00207"><span>Dugan et al.</span></a><span>. They give a weight construction for gated one-hidden-layer MLPs</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. The gating weights </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>are chosen at random. The requirement that every key hit its target output (margin-optimal label directions, compressed to roughly </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> dimensions with a random projection that the output matrix then undoes) then becomes a linear system in the entries of the up-projection matrix </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, which they solve exactly.</span></p><p><span>We have some preliminary results for adapting this construction to our setting that indicate it performs better than our current hand-coded solution or even our hybrid solution, though it still falls well short of the trained one. The reason we haven't switched over to their construction yet is that it seems to us very unlike something models trained with gradient descent would or could learn. A bit like our own construction in Appendix A. We do ultimately want to reverse engineer trained models, getting storage efficiency up is just a stepping stone for that. Nevertheless, adapting this construction would be a valid challenge entry and we'd probably have included an adaptation of their algorithm in this post if we'd found it earlier.</span></p><h1><span>Our attempt</span></h1><p><span>This is our</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-6">[6]</a></sup></span><span>attempt at solving the challenge we proposed above. Below we present first our algorithm, and then how it performed relative to trained models.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>At </span><b><span>acc ≥ 0.9</span></b><span>, our fully hand-coded models scale almost as well as the trained models (fitted exponent 1.93 vs 1.97), but with a worse prefactor.</span></li><li value="2"><span>At </span><b><span>acc = 1</span></b><span>, the hand-coded models fall further behind as models grow: about 27× fewer facts than trained models at </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, with the gap widening.</span></li><li value="3"><span>The </span><b><span>hybrid</span></b><span> models (hand-coded embeddings, trained unembedding) scale about as well as the fully trained solution for both acc ≥ 0.9 and acc = 1 (fitted exponents 2.04, 2.09 vs. 1.97, 2.04).</span></li></ul><p><span>You can find the code for our construction </span><a href="https://github.com/LindaLinsefors/Memory-Toy-Models/blob/master/hand_coded_models/hc2.py"><span>here</span></a><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-21"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-21">[20]</a></sup></span></p><h2><span>Algorithm: Summary</span></h2><p><span>First we associate each label with a unique set of neurons.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-22"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-22">[21]</a></sup></span><span>These neurons will somehow identify facts with this label.</span></p><p><span>It would be great if, given a fact with label</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, all the neurons associated with label </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>were active, and no other neurons are active. However for most sets of facts, this will not be possible.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-23"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-23">[22]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>An alternative, which is less ideal, but has the significant advantage of being possible, is to set up the embedding weights such that, for any input fact with any label</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, all neurons associated with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>will be active. Some additional neurons will also be active, but not all of them. With some skill and some luck, none of the wrong labels will have all their neurons activated.</span></p><p><span>However, there is one more problem. "Active" isn't a single number. Even if the correct label has all its neurons active, and none of the incorrect labels has all of their neurons active, an incorrect label can still have higher overall activation, if that label's neurons activate more strongly. One way to solve this is to make sure all active neurons have the exact same value, but that would add an unnecessary constraint. A better solution is to flip the script, and make sure that all neurons associated with label</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> are </span><i><span>inactive</span></i><span> for any fact with label </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. This exploits the one place where a ReLU network gives us exact equality for free: every negative pre-activation is mapped to exactly zero. "All of this label's neurons are inactive" is therefore a condition the readout can check exactly, by giving those neurons negative weight into the label's logit and checking for a logit of exactly zero.</span></p><p><span>In summary:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Assign a unique set of neurons to each label.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Choose the embedding weights such that, for every label</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and every fact with label </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, all pre-activations will be less than or equal to zero, for every neuron associated with label </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Under the above constraint, try to make as many pre-activations as possible be above zero.</span></li><li value="4"><span>For every label</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, assign a constant negative weight between the logit for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and every neuron associated with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. Let all other unembedding weights be zero.</span></li></ul><h2><span>Algorithm: More details</span></h2><p><span>Our algorithm for assigning weight matrix values has these steps:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Assign </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> ReLU neurons to each label. This means that each neuron will be assigned to several labels. These assignments should achieve both of: each neuron should have approximately the same number of labels assigned to it as any other neuron; the max neuron overlap between any pair of labels should be as small as possible.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Choose the embedding weights such that each ReLU neuron assigned to label</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> will output zero for all facts with label </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Assign negative weights going from ReLU neurons assigned to label</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, to the logit for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Hyperparameter sweep: Repeat the above for different values of the construction's two hyperparameters, to find the best ones.</span></li></ul><h3><span>Assigning neurons to labels</span></h3><p><span>There are</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> ReLU neurons, and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> labels. Each label gets assigned </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> neurons. In most of our experiments </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, which means for any </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, the assignments will overlap.</span></p><p><span>One problem our network needs to solve is that there will likely be some pattern of facts</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span class="math-tex"></span><span> -&gt; </span><span class="math-tex"></span></li><li value="2"><span class="math-tex"></span><span> -&gt; </span><span class="math-tex"></span></li><li value="3"><span class="math-tex"></span><span> -&gt; not </span><span class="math-tex"></span></li><li value="4"><span class="math-tex"></span><span> -&gt; not </span><span class="math-tex"></span></li></ul><p><span>Any weight allocation, on this model architecture, where the logit for some label only depends on a single ReLU neuron, will fail at encoding this pattern. Therefore, the network either needs more ReLU neurons than labels (not realistic) or the labels will have to somehow share neurons, i.e. some sort of </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2022/toy_model/index.html"><span>superposition</span></a><span> encoding.</span></p><p><span>We don't know a priori what the best value of</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is, so </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is given as a hyperparameter, to search over later. Given </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, we want to find an allocation where the assignments are spread out nicely. I.e. we want all neurons to be used by approximately the same number of labels, and we want to minimize the max neuron overlap between any pair of labels.</span></p><p><span>We had Claude Code write the script that does this, and verified that the outputs look good. There are probably many ways to achieve similar allocations, and we can't think of any reason why the exact method matters, so we will not go into this further. If you want to look into the details, see </span><a href="https://github.com/LindaLinsefors/Memory-Toy-Models/blob/master/hand_coded_models/hc2.py"><span>the code</span></a><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-21"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-21">[20]</a></sup></span></p><h3><span>Embedding weights</span></h3><p><span>The next step is to make sure that every ReLU neuron always output zero on all facts with a label assigned to that neuron. Recall that in this architecture (Figure 4), the embedding weights map input tokens directly to neuron pre-activations, so "embedding weight" below means the weight from a token (at position one or two) to a neuron.</span></p><p><span>In broad strokes, our algorithm for each neuron is:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>We list all facts with a label that is assigned to that neuron.</span></li><li value="2"><span>For the first input token, we count how many times each token appears in the list of facts, take the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> most frequent of these input tokens and assign them </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>for that neuron.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Repeat step 2 for the second input token.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Find any facts in the list where neither token got a weight of -1 in step 2 or 3, and assign </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> to both of that fact's input tokens.</span></li><li value="5"><span>Assign </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> to all remaining input tokens.</span></li></ol><h3><span>Unembedding weights</span></h3><p><span>We set the unembedding weights between each label and its assigned neurons to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. We set all other unembedding weights to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>We did also try assigning positive values everywhere else, but for the success criterion we use (looking at argmax of the logits), adding these positive values makes no difference. We first noticed this empirically, but it's also a mathematical fact.</span></p><h3><span>Hybrid model: Half hand-coded, half learned</span></h3><p><span>Same as above except the unembedding weights are randomly initialized and then trained.</span></p><h3><span>Hyperparameter search over S and top_fraction</span></h3><p><span>When testing the capacity of this model design, we always do a hyperparameter search over</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>For the hand-coded models the winning </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is typically </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>is the model dimension. For hybrid models, this number is a bit smaller.</span></p><p><span>For the hand-coded models the winning </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is typically in the range </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. For the hybrid models the number is a bit larger.</span></p><p><span>See Appendix C and D for details.</span></p><h2><span>Results</span></h2><p><span>The plot below shows our data from binary search to find the maximum number of facts a model can learn.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>The model architecture is the one shown in Fig 4, for all models.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>trained</span></b><span>: All weights are learned.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>hybrid</span></b><span>: Embedding weights are selected according to our algorithm, and unembedding weights are trained.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>hand-coded</span></b><span>: All weights are selected according to our algorithm.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span>rand-emb</span></b><span>: Embedding weights are randomly initialized and frozen. Unembedding weights are trained.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-25"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-25">[23]</a></sup></span></li><li value="6"><span>Training (when applicable) is done with Adam, lr=1e-2, up to 5000 epochs with early stopping if 100% accuracy is reached, or if accuracy has not improved for 100 epochs.</span></li><li value="7"><span>All models are evaluated on accuracy, by which we mean percentage of facts the model correctly predicts. This is calculated as </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></li><li value="8"><b><span>acc </span></b><span class="math-tex"></span><b><span> 0.9</span></b><span> means that the accuracy has to be at least 90% for the model to count as successful.</span></li><li value="9"><b><span>acc = 1</span></b><span> means that accuracy has to be 100% for the model to count as successful.</span></li><li value="10"><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the size of the model. </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-10"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-10">[10]</a></sup></span></li></ul><figure class="image"><img alt="scaling_any_log.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784833676/lexical_client_uploads/agrhtd60o1ilgmgxj5sd.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 5: </span></b><span>Maximum number of facts vs model dimension.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>The best-fit lines from Figure 5:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-26"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-26">[24]</a></sup></span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>acc ≥ 0.9</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>trained</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span class="table-cell"><p><span>hybrid</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span
