/></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>rand-emb</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><h2><span>Takeaways</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><span>We match the trained model's scaling behaviour for acc=0.9, though with a worse prefactor</span></li><li value="2"><span>For acc=1.0 we don't scale as well as the trained model, which indicates there's probably some kind of flaw in our algorithm.</span></li><li value="3"><span>The hybrid model that trains only the unembedding matrix significantly outperforms the rand-emb model, indicating that our embedding matrix works a lot better than these random uniform weights. However, the random uniform matrix has a scaling exponent somewhat notably larger than </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, indicating that it might get less inefficient at larger scales.</span></li><li value="4"><span>The hybrid model also significantly outperforms our hand-coded model, indicating there's some room left to improve our construction with a better unembedding matrix. We think designing good unembedding matrices is probably easier than embedding matrices, since it's mostly just a sort of linear classification problem. So there's maybe some low-hanging fruit left to pick to improve our construction here.</span></li></ul><h2><span>Do the matrices look similar?</span></h2><p><span>There might be multiple different algorithms to achieve high memorisation capacity, so even if we match the trained model's performance that doesn't necessarily mean we understand how the trained model works yet, though we think it'd be a good intermediate result. That said, how close do the weight matrices our algorithm constructs look to the trained weight matrices?</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="68747470733a2f2f6861636b6d642e696f2f5f75706c6f6164732f537973725f4332347a6c2e706e67.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784826818/lexical_client_uploads/ac0dnuamgcrwpetarnhq.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 6</span></b></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>The first row shows our hand coded weights. The second and third rows are weights for models trained on the same number of facts as the hand-coded ones, and as many as they can fit respectively. </span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="68747470733a2f2f6861636b6d642e696f2f5f75706c6f6164732f53794c324177794866652e706e67.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784826981/lexical_client_uploads/v50w6yhohdeunuvnav3u.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 7</span></b></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Some surface-level observations: Our embedding weights only take three different values, 1, 0 or -1. The network's weights are more smeared out, though the ones in the second row are quite trimodal, with one narrow cluster at zero and two broader clusters of positive and negative weights. The weights in the third row are much more spread out. They could maybe be three broad peaks grown together?</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-27"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-27">[25]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>The unembedding is pretty different even in the second row, with our hand-coded weights being bimodal whereas the trained weights are trimodal again, with a small peak at zero and big positive and negative peaks.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="68747470733a2f2f6861636b6d642e696f2f5f75706c6f6164732f4879455f644332347a672e706e67.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827013/lexical_client_uploads/umo42dsao9ynlevw0rkl.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 8</span></b></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>For the neurons, while our hand-coded algorithm stores facts as patterns of inactive neurons, trained models seem to use patterns of active neurons instead. Maybe as a result of that, the proportions of positive vs. zero activations appear to be somewhat flipped. The trained models' neuron activations are also more smeared out and gradual than ours, as one would expect from their more smeared out embedding weights.</span></p><h1><span>Contributions and acknowledgements</span></h1><p><span>Linda did most of the work. Lucius gave advice and encouragement, came up with the alternative construction in Appendix A, and helped write and edit the post.</span></p><p><span>Thanks to Nico Penttilä and Mikhail Doroshenko for suggesting the Uniform Attention variant.</span></p><p><span>Linda is supported by a grant from Coefficient Giving. Lucius works at Goodfire AI.</span></p><h1><span>Appendix A: Near-optimal construction for </span><span class="math-tex"></span></h1><p><span>Throughout the main text the hidden width </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is scaled to stay </span><i><span>b</span></i><span>elow* the input vocabulary size (we use </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>). This appendix treats the boundary case just past that constraint. The hidden layer is given one neuron more than the input vocabulary size,</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. In this case a hand-coded construction can store all </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>possible facts with 100% accuracy. It uses the same architecture as Figure 4 (two embedding matrices, one ReLU layer, an unembedding; no norms, residuals, or bias terms</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-28"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-28">[26]</a></sup></span><span>).</span></p><p><span>This algorithm does seem qualitatively very unlike the ones trained models learn, with only two active neurons per fact. So it might be a bit of a dead end. We nevertheless include it in case there are ways to adapt ideas from it for future constructions.</span></p><p><span>Let</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> be the label of the fact with input </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>The first</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> neurons will be </span><b><span>selector neurons</span></b><span>, with neuron </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> switching on only if first-position token </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>is active, and the final neuron is an always-on bias. The weights are:</span></p><div></div><div></div><div></div><p><span>The overall scale of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is free: multiplying it by any positive constant leaves the argmax unchanged but makes the softmax arbitrarily confident in the correct label.</span></p><p><span>In words: the first token uses the large negative weight </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> to switch every selector neuron except the one it is assigned to off. The second token's embedding then writes the fact's label (shifted up by one) into that surviving selector neuron as its activation value. The</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> shift keeps the value positive, so that a label of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>is distinguishable from a silent neuron. The bias neuron is wired to be permanently on and supplies the per-class thresholds that are required to read the labels out linearly (which is why we cannot fold it away in a bias-free architecture, and why we need the extra neuron).</span></p><p><b><span>How it works.</span></b><span> Take the fact</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. On input </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, selector neuron </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> has pre-activation </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and fires with value </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>; every other selector neuron </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> has pre-activation </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and stays silent; the bias neuron is </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. In general, writing </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>,</span></p><div></div><div></div><div></div><p><span>The bias neuron's output weights </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> make the activations for different labels linearly separable. The labels</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>closest to the neuron's activation value then always gets the highest logit. This holds for every one of the</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> pairs, so any set of up to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>possible facts is memorized perfectly.</span></p><h1><span>Appendix B: More on what parts of the model matter</span></h1><p><span>The point of this experiment is to figure out which parts of the network are important for the sequence memorization task, so that we know which parts are safe to ignore or even remove, in order to make understanding the model easier.</span></p><p><span>What we found was that having an MLP is the most important part (approximately responsible for learning half of the facts), and everything else only matters a bit.</span></p><p><span>We trained all different versions of the toy model, to see how many facts each of them could learn. There are some patterns, but unfortunately, for most of them, we can't separate what is a result of changing the expressivity of the model architecture and what is a result of making it easier or harder for Adam to learn good solutions.</span></p><p><span>For this experiment, we used a single model size across all architectures:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span class="math-tex"></span></li><li value="2"><span class="math-tex"></span></li><li value="3"><span class="math-tex"></span></li><li value="4"><span class="math-tex"></span></li></ul><p><span>We say that a model has "learned a fact" if, when the model is given the first two tokens of this sequence, it correctly predicts the third token. And by "correctly predicts" we mean that argmaxing over the logits locates the correct output token.</span></p><p><span>To find the maximum number of facts a model can learn, we performed a binary search over the number of facts, to find the highest number of facts such that the model learned all of them.</span></p><p><span>Inside the binary search, for each number of facts tested, we trained 11 models in parallel, with the exact same facts but differently randomly initialized weights. We used three different success criteria — "Any", "Most" and "All" — meaning that we counted the model as having succeeded at learning all the facts if it succeeded in any, most, or all of the 11 trials.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-29"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-29">[27]</a></sup></span></p><p><i><b><span>All tables (below) show all of "Any", "Most" and "All". However, the analysis in the text, and in the main post, is based on the results for "Any", since that's the most stable setting.</span></b></i></p><p><span>Each individual training run was done as follows. We used Adam with no weight decay, and with all facts included in every batch.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-30"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-30">[28]</a></sup></span><span>Each model was initially trained with learning rate </span><code><span>lr=1e-2</span></code><span>, for up to 50,000 epochs, or until early stopping due to reaching full accuracy, or accuracy not improving for the last 5,000 epochs. If after this step the accuracy is less than 100% but above 95%, then training continues with </span><code><span>lr=3e-3</span></code><span>, with the same stopping criterion. If still not at 100% accuracy, training continues with </span><code><span>lr=1e-3</span></code><span>, with the same stopping criterion.</span></p><p><span>For each architecture and each of Any/Most/All we ran a binary search to find the maximum number of facts it could learn. Furthermore, we repeated each such binary search 4 times, to check for stability. The "Any" setting had the highest stability (similar max number of facts over all 4 duplicate experiments), and "All" had the worst stability.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-31"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-31">[29]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>All the results for all the experiments are shown in the table below.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="table_E5.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827065/lexical_client_uploads/sq1dyqfaqct1u9lkkflv.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Table B.1: </span></b><span>Maximum facts memorized for each model architecture. Within each group the row-wise maximum is shown in bold and values more than 20% from the group's median are boxed as outliers. </span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Note that 1024 is the dataset ceiling, so configurations reaching it have saturated the data rather than the model.</span></p><p><span>To see the effect of each of Mixing, Norms, Res, Bias and Act, we'll look at pairs (or triples for Mixing) of model architectures that are the same except for that variable. E.g. for Norms, we look at every pair that differs only in whether it has norms or not, to see how much models with norms typically outperform the ones without norms. We're doing this analysis on the "Any" runs, since these have the most stable outcomes.</span></p><p><span>However, before doing all that, it's worth noting one major outlier.</span></p><h3><span>MLP + Norms + No Residual around MLP + No Bias + ReLU</span></h3><p><span>For any form of mixing (learned attention, uniform attention, or dual embedding), networks with </span><b><span>MLP</span></b><span>=✅, </span><b><span>Norm</span></b><span>=✅, </span><b><span>Res</span></b><span>=❌, </span><b><span>Bias</span></b><span>=❌ and </span><b><span>Act</span></b><span>=</span><b><span>ReLU</span></b><span> do really badly. Almost as badly as (and in one case slightly worse than) removing the MLP.</span></p><p><span>This combination is extra bad for some reason that isn't just the sum of its parts. We don't know why. Specifically, we don't know if the limitation is due to training dynamics or due to what is possible for this architecture.</span></p><p><span>Instead of writing "except for </span><b><span>MLP</span></b><span>=✅, </span><b><span>Norm</span></b><span>=✅, </span><b><span>Res</span></b><span>=❌, </span><b><span>Bias</span></b><span>=❌ </span><b><span>Act</span></b><span>=</span><b><span>ReLU</span></b><span>" in every subsection below, we'll just point this out here. Having pointed this out, this data will be excluded from the triple or pairwise comparisons below.</span></p><h2><span>Triple or pairwise comparisons</span></h2><p><span>Now back to triple and pairwise comparisons. I.e., we compare outcomes (number of learned facts) for pairs (or triples, when varying Mixing) of model architectures where the only difference is a single setting.</span></p><h3><span>Mixing</span></h3><figure class="image"><img alt="table_attention.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827125/lexical_client_uploads/ffz8qxj7xxsk7yju6cr8.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Table B.2: </span></b><span>Dual embedding vs. uniform attention vs. full attention. The first set of columns shows the settings of the other architecture variables. 2E&gt;U is the number of replications where 2Emb outperformed Unif Attn. The first %</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the average percentage more facts 2Emb learns than Unif Attn. </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the average number of facts 2Emb learns beyond Unif Attn. The next three columns are the same, except comparing Unif Attn with Lrn Attn. The last three columns are the same, except comparing 2Emb with Lrn Attn.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><ul><li value="1"><span>When </span><b><span>Norms</span></b><span>=❌ and </span><b><span>MLP</span></b><span>=❌, i.e. there is nothing but embedding, possibly attention, and unembedding — in this case only — 2Emb, Unif Attn and Lrn Attn do equally well.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-32"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-32">[30]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span>For all other settings </span><b><span>2Emb</span></b><span> does better than </span><b><span>Unif Attn</span></b><span>, which does better than </span><b><span>Lrn Attn</span></b><span>.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Going from </span><b><span>Unif Attn</span></b><span> to </span><b><span>2Emb</span></b><span> lets the network learn </span><b><span>74 - 176 (7.8% - 23.9%)</span></b><span> more facts.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Going from </span><b><span>Lrn Attn</span></b><span> to </span><b><span>Unif Attn</span></b><span> lets the network learn </span><b><span>46 - 222 (7.9% - 39%)</span></b><span> more facts.</span></li></ul><p><span>It is notable that Mixing (the setting that determines the embedding and attention) has the least effect precisely when everything else is turned off, i.e. when there is only the embedding, possibly attention, and the unembedding.</span></p><p><span>It's not surprising that dual embedding does the best, since this architecture is (arguably) the most powerful. Because attention is non-linear and the dual embedding is linear, there are things that the attention can express that the dual embedding can't. But on the other hand, the dual embedding gets to encode the input for each token position entirely separately, which gives the network more freedom. Additionally, this no-attention setup should be easier to train, since it's simpler.</span></p><p><span>More surprising is that uniform attention outperforms learned attention, given that learned attention is strictly more powerful. Therefore, this has to be because of ease of training. This interpretation is also supported by the observation that the number of facts networks with learned attention manage to learn is unstable. You can see this in the number of outliers in Table B.1, and also in how much the number of facts drops from Any to Most to All.</span></p><h3><span>MLP</span></h3><figure class="image"><img alt="table_ff.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827174/lexical_client_uploads/wdunhw5khlsxueanygbc.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Table B.3:</span></b><span> MLP=✅ vs MLP=❌. The first set of columns shows the settings of the other architecture variables. 'On' is the number of replications where MLP=✅ outperformed MLP=❌. '=' is the number of replications where both did equally well. 'Off' is the number of replications where MLP=❌ outperformed MLP=✅. Mean%</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the average percentage more facts MLP=✅ learns than MLP=❌. </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the average number of facts MLP=✅ learns beyond MLP=❌.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Adding an </span><b><span>MLP</span></b><span> block lets the network learn </span><b><span>324 - 794 (60% - 373%)</span></b><span> more facts.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Adding an </span><b><span>MLP</span></b><span> block makes the </span><b><span>biggest</span></b><span> difference when </span><b><span>Mixing</span></b><span>={</span><b><span>2Emb, Unif Attn</span></b><span>} and </span><b><span>Norms</span></b><span>=❌. This is probably because in this setting the MLP's ReLU or GELU neurons are the only non-linearities.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Adding an MLP block makes the </span><b><span>smallest</span></b><span> difference when </span><b><span>Mixing</span></b><span>=</span><b><span>2Emb</span></b><span> and </span><b><span>Norms</span></b><span>=✅. However, this is probably just a ceiling effect, since the model with the MLP maxed out the dataset for this setting.</span></li></ul><p><span>Not surprisingly, adding the MLP makes the biggest difference to the number of learnable facts out of any of the settings.</span></p><h3><span>Norms</span></h3><figure class="image"><img alt="table_norm.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827211/lexical_client_uploads/dxwpnyniiespkoxtbxi9.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Table B.4:</span></b><span> Norms=✅ vs Norms=❌. The first set of columns shows the settings of the other architecture variables. 'On' is the number of replications where Norms=✅ outperformed Norms=❌. '=' is the number of replications where both did equally well. 'Off' is the number of replications where Norms=❌ outperformed Norms=✅. Mean%</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the average percentage more facts Norms=✅ learns than Norms=❌. </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the average number of facts Norms=✅ learns beyond Norms=❌.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><ul><li value="1"><span>When </span><b><span>MLP</span></b><span>=❌, adding norms lets the network learn </span><b><span>162 - 362 (79% - 174%)</span></b><span> more facts. This effect is </span><i><span>largest</span></i><span> for </span><b><span>Mixing</span></b><span> = </span><b><span>2Emb</span></b><span>.</span></li><li value="2"><span>When </span><b><span>MLP</span></b><span>=✅, adding norms lets the network learn </span><b><span>22 - 240 (2.2% - 42%)</span></b><span> more facts. The effect is </span><i><span>largest</span></i><span> for </span><b><span>Mixing</span></b><span> = </span><b><span>Lrn Attn</span></b><span>.</span></li></ul><p><span>Norms are generally useful for learning more facts. Norms make a bigger difference if there is attention, and if there is no MLP. Possibly this means that the norms in front of the attention and unembedding are helpful, while the norm in front of the MLP is anti-helpful. Or possibly the MLP and norms overlap somewhat in function, such that the MLP makes the norms less useful.</span></p><p><span>The fact that the MLP has its biggest effect when there are no other non-linearities in the network points to the overlapping-function hypothesis. However, the unusually bad performance of </span><b><span>MLP</span></b><span>=✅, </span><b><span>Norms</span></b><span>=✅, </span><b><span>Res</span></b><span>=❌, </span><b><span>Bias</span></b><span>=❌, </span><b><span>Act</span></b><span>=</span><b><span>ReLU</span></b><span> might be a sign that adding norms can be bad for the performance of the MLP.</span></p><h3><span>Residual Connection around the MLP</span></h3><figure class="image"><img alt="table_residual.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827249/lexical_client_uploads/txadh5kb0xcft5biwkzj.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Table B.5: </span></b><span>Res=✅ vs Res=❌. The first set of columns shows the settings of the other architecture variables. 'On' is the number of replications where Res=✅ outperformed Res=❌. '=' is the number of replications where both did equally well. 'Off' is the number of replications where Res=❌ outperformed Res=✅. Mean%</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the average percentage more facts Res=✅ learns than Res=❌. </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the average number of facts Res=✅ learns beyond Res=❌.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Adding this residual connection lets the network learn </span><b><span>-8 to 244 (-0.7% to 34%)</span></b><span> more facts.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The only setting where adding this residual is bad for the network is </span><b><span>Mixing</span></b><span>=</span><b><span>Lrn Attn</span></b><span>, </span><b><span>Norms</span></b><span>=❌, </span><b><span>Bias</span></b><span>=✅, </span><b><span>Act</span></b><span>=</span><b><span>ReLU</span></b><span>. But the effect is tiny, </span><b><span>8 facts (0.7%)</span></b><span>, so it's probably just a fluke.</span></li></ul><h3><span>MLP Bias</span></h3><figure class="image"><img alt="table_bias.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827275/lexical_client_uploads/d3glvaxqhsbsoxljodtz.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Table B.6: </span></b><span>Bias=✅ vs Bias=❌. The first set of columns shows the settings of the other architecture variables. 'On' is the number of replications where Bias=✅ outperformed Bias=❌. '=' is the number of replications where both did equally well. 'Off' is the number of replications where Bias=❌ outperformed Bias=✅. Mean%</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the average percentage more facts Bias=✅ learns than Bias=❌. </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the average number of facts Bias=✅ learns beyond Bias=❌.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><span>Adding a bias ought to be strictly helpful, but for some reason it's anti-helpful in a few cases.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Adding a bias to the MLP lets the network learn </span><b><span>-52 to 156 (-5.1% to 22%)</span></b><span> more facts.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Adding an MLP bias performs at its worst when </span><b><span>Norms</span></b><span>=❌ and </span><b><span>Res</span></b><span>=✅. Given this setting, adding the bias lets the network learn </span><b><span>-52 to 14 (-5.1% to 1.4%)</span></b><span> more facts.</span></li></ul><h3><span>Activation Function</span></h3><figure class="image"><img alt="table_activation.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827304/lexical_client_uploads/mglf1b9lommbwjl8i9vw.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Table B.7:</span></b><span> GELU vs ReLU. The first set of columns shows the settings of the other architecture variables. 'G&gt;R' is the number of replications where GELU outperformed ReLU. '=' is the number of replications where both did equally well. 'R&gt;G' is the number of replications where ReLU outperformed GELU. Mean%</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the average percentage more facts GELU learns than ReLU. </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the average number of facts GELU learns beyond ReLU.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><b><span>GELU</span></b><span> is typically better than </span><b><span>ReLU</span></b><span>, but the difference is small.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Changing from </span><b><span>ReLU</span></b><span> to </span><b><span>GELU</span></b><span> lets the network learn </span><b><span>-6 to 78 (-0.2% to +12.7%)</span></b><span> more facts.</span></li></ul><h1><span>Appendix C: More on Scaling</span></h1><p><span>The data points in Figures 3 and 5 in the main post are for the "Any" condition. Below are the same two plots with "Most" and "All" included.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="scaling_only_trained.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827349/lexical_client_uploads/xipogqkjbeb5qjqgzyd6.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure C.1:</span></b><span> Same as Figure 3, but with "Most" and "All" settings included. The fitted lines include all data points for each of "simple" or "full", aggregating over "any"/"most"/"all".</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="scaling.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827619/lexical_client_uploads/dbfc9mia0vvjfcs0ba56.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure C.2:</span></b><span> Same as Figure 5 in the main post, but with "Most" and "All" settings included. The fitted lines include all data points for each condition, aggregating over "any"/"most"/"all".</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><h1><span>Appendix D: Best S (number of neurons per label) for the hand-coded and hybrid models</span></h1><p><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is (at least for the hand-coded model) the number of neurons used by any label. We're interested in how this scales with various model parameters, since it might give us some clue about what we should expect superposition to look like over ReLU and ReLU-like neurons. To be clear, anything we see here is at best a small hint, with no guarantee of having anything to do with how computations are distributed in fully trained models. But it's still a little bit of Bayesian evidence, and maybe if it meets up with other evidence later on, it will tell us something. This is why we think it's worth recording.</span></p><p><span>In the experiments in the main post, in order to get the best version of our hand-coded model, we did a hyperparameter sweep over</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. From this we can extract the optimal </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> for different model sizes by looking at </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> from the winning (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>) pair.</span></p><p><span>In the scaling experiments (see main post) we investigated models with dimensions</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. Looking at the best-performing </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> from these runs, we find that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><img alt="appendixB_S_vs_d.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827679/lexical_client_uploads/chg9fyq608yvc8llasca.png" /><p><span>However, in these experiments the relations between</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>are locked together, i.e. we can't tell from the data which of them influences the ideal value of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>In the next experiment we did a binary search for the max number of facts for every combination of the following parameters:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span class="math-tex"></span></li><li value="2"><span class="math-tex"></span></li><li value="3"><span class="math-tex"></span></li><li value="4"><span>accuracy requirement </span><span class="math-tex"></span></li><li value="5"><span>success aggregation </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> {</span><i><span>any, most, all</span></i><span>}</span></li><li value="6"><span>model type </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> {</span><i><span>hand-coded, hybrid</span></i><span>}</span></li></ul><p><span>Below you can see how the optimal </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> depends on all of them.</span></p><p><span>Note that we swept over </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. A small number of runs may have hit the ceiling, i.e. the optimal value of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is actually something above </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><br /></p><img alt="appendixB_S_vs_d_hc.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827728/lexical_client_uploads/uzct66hzmxrb6izm58g0.png" /><p><br /></p><img alt="appendixB_S_vs_all_hc.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827739/lexical_client_uploads/xd8vw8uirmrfsnmdfruk.png" /><p><br /></p><img alt="appendixB_S_vs_d_hy.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827747/lexical_client_uploads/cpyypfewytmbqocilyo3.png" /><p><br /></p><img alt="appendixB_S_vs_all_hy.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827754/lexical_client_uploads/v2dnks3mc8qcaw3e2djf.png" /><p><span>As you can see, the picture is less clean when the different model dimensions are varied independently. Just scaling up the hidden layer increases the optimal</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> faster than </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. But </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> also decreases with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, just enough to add up to the pattern we see in the first figure of this appendix when they increase together.</span></p><p><span>In the hybrid model, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> also plays a role.</span></p><h1><span>Appendix E: Best "top_fraction" for the hand-coded and hybrid models</span></h1><p><span>The other hyperparameter (besides </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>) used when creating the embedding matrix for the hand-coded and hybrid models is a variable we dubbed </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. See the main post for the definition.</span></p><p><i><b><span>We don't expect that looking into this variable will tell you anything interesting. We don't recommend paying attention to this section unless you know something we don't.</span></b></i></p><p><span>But for completeness, and because it cost us almost no extra work to add this, here are the same plots as in Appendix D, but for</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> instead of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>Note that we only swept over </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. Some runs with the hybrid model seem to have hit the ceiling, i.e. the real best</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is something above </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><br /></p><img alt="appendixC_f_vs_d.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827826/lexical_client_uploads/uryoelegsejimssmw6pg.png" /><p><br /></p><img alt="appendixC_f_vs_d_hc.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827831/lexical_client_uploads/ejnmwo346krdoenrxaan.png" /><p><br /></p><img alt="appendixC_f_vs_all_hc.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827873/lexical_client_uploads/rmeutjzdxqviz8z3nspn.png" /><p><br /></p><img alt="appendixC_f_vs_d_hy.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827895/lexical_client_uploads/cwtpxxf8qnot1zshd3gw.png" /><p><br /></p><img alt="appendixC_f_vs_all_hy.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784827909/lexical_client_uploads/jq36urfcqbclrpmby4l2.png" /><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-1"><p><span>Meaning the model memorises the correct label for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> of the facts it is trained on. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-1"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-2"><p><span>Up to a log factor that comes from larger output label dictionaries needing more bits to index. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-2"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-3"><p><span>Not a trivial one though. We couldn't find an existing analytic solution for linear classification under softmax cross entropy loss, or linear classification under argmax. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-3"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-4"><p><span>Importantly, the MLP's hidden layer size </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> here is smaller than the input token dictionary </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. For the case</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (or </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>if we get output biases), we already have a more efficient construction, see Appendix A. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-4"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-5"><p><span>An even simpler example of lookup, not studied in this post, is </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2021/framework/index.html#zero-layer-transformers"><span>bi-gram statistics, which is (at least sometimes) encoded in the embedding + unembedding matrices</span></a><span>. Bi-grams don't need any token-combination machinery, which is why they can live entirely in the embedding/unembedding. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-5"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-6"><p><b><span>Lucius:</span></b><span> Well, Linda's attempt, really. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-6"><span>↩︎</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-6:1"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-7"><p><span>We used a fixed random seed when generating facts in order to avoid some runs getting lucky and getting easier facts, and to specifically have the same facts for trained networks and hand-coded networks. The last part kind of failed because torch random functions give different results when run on CPU vs GPU, even when the seed is the same. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-7"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-8"><p><span>Thanks to Nico Penttilä and Mikhail Doroshenko for suggesting this variant. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-8"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-9"><p><span>If the bias is included, that means both the linear projection into and the linear projection out of the ReLU/GELU neurons get a bias. (Making them actually not linear functions but affine functions, in strict math terminology.) If the bias is not included, this means neither of them get a bias. All other linear connections in the rest of the network (e.g. embeddings, etc) are always bias-free. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-9"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-10"><p><span>Initially </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> was the same as all the other values, but too many networks maxed out the number of possible facts, so we doubled </span><span class="math-tex"></span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-10"><span>↩︎</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-10:1"><span>↩︎</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-10:2"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-11"><p><span>Adam to be specific, with the following learning rate schedule: We start out with lr=1e-2, and train for either 50000 epochs or until accuracy [defined as </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>] has not improved for 5000 epochs. If at the end of this accuracy is below 1 but above 0.95, we continue the training with lr dropped to 3e-3 and same stopping criteria, and finally repeat for lr=1e-3. Training also ends if the accuracy=1 is reached. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-11"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-12"><p><span>If there is an MLP block then Mixing is generally more important than norms, and the other way round without the MLP. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-12"><span>↩︎</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-12:1"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-13"><p><span>Strictly more expressive because: with uniform attention, the residual stream at the second position is </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> plus positional terms: Two per-position linear maps that are constrained to share the same embedding matrix </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. 2Emb replaces these with two fully independent matrices, so it can represent anything uniform attention can, and more. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-13"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-14"><p><span>With some rotation added to the first token embedding so as to be able to tell apart inputs with the two tokens swapped, e.g. to differentiate the input "1,2" from the input "2,1". </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-14"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-15"><p><span>Just turning off the norms or turning on the MLP bias or switching from ReLU to GELU (changes that normally have small effects) is sufficient to restore this architecture to the normal capabilities range. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-15"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-16"><p><span>That is, if you want to be able to compare your results with ours. But if you make progress on this challenge using some other architecture, we'd be interested in that too. Just make sure to include something like an MLP layer, since that is where most of the sequence memorization capacity lives in the trained models. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-16"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-17"><p><span>Except for the hybrid condition where you can use gradient descent-esque methods for the unembedding weights. See "Evaluation Criteria". </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-17"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-18"><p><span>There are four combinations:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>acc = 1</span></li><li value="2"><span>acc &gt;= 0.9</span></li><li value="3"><span>hybrid, acc = 1,</span></li><li value="4"><span>hybrid, acc &gt;= 0.9</span></li></ul><p><span> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-18"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-19"><p><span>Meaning no k+1 of the points lie on a common affine hyperplane. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-19"><span>↩︎</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-19:1"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-21"><p><span>I (Linda) cleaned this one file in the repo. Everything else there is a mess. I don't recommend trying to read any other file. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-21"><span>↩︎</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-21:1"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-22"><p><span>To clarify: Typically each neuron will be used by multiple labels, but no two labels share all their neurons. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-22"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-23"><p><span>Given some set of facts (i.e. all the facts with label </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, or all the facts associated with any label that is associated with neuron </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>), you typically can't decide that some neuron </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> will be active (or inactive) for only that set of facts, and no others. That's only possible if the inputs to those facts are linearly separable, which is typically not the case. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-23"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-25"><p><span>The embedding matrix weights are drawn from a uniform distribution centered on zero. Specifically it's uniform over </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. However, the scale should not matter, since that can be compensated for by the unembedding weights. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-25"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-26"><p><span>Note that the prefactors here are a little different from the ones in Figure 0 because we're fitting both the prefactors and the exponents to confirm which exponents seem close to the desired </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. Any exponents </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> also shouldn't be taken too seriously. They're either noise in the fit or ought to vanish at large enough </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-26"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-27"><p><span>The absolute scale of the weights doesn't matter that much because ReLUs are scale invariant. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-27"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-28"><p><span>If we have output biases, we can adjust the construction to only need </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-28"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-29"><p><span>"Most" means that there are more successes than failures, i.e. at least 6 out of 11. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-29"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-30"><p><span>I.e. batches and epochs are the same thing. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-30"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-31"><p><span>It's not surprising that "All" had bad stability, since it only takes one bad run to throw off the entire batch. But it was not a priori obvious to us that "Any" would be more stable than "Most". </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-31"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-32"><p><b><span>2Emb</span></b><span> and </span><b><span>Unif Attn</span></b><span> learn </span><b><span>208</span></b><span> facts in each of the four repeated experiments. Lrn Attn learns </span><b><span>208</span></b><span> in two of the replications, slightly more in one, and slightly less in one. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-32"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnp3ess17pym"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefp3ess17pym">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-20"><p><span>See the appendix for our much less 'realistic' but much more efficient storage construction in the easier </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> setting. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-20"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-24"><p><span>This could have been any negative number, and it would work equally well. It just happens to be -2 in the code. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-jXdzaFrLEj5wYD5bh-24"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KWtchKwwnJkd4bwCi/challenge-hand-coding-weights-for-efficient-sequence-1#comments">Discuss</a>

### [The OpenAI/Huggingface incident | Redwood Research podcast episode 2](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9auCLJg3Z77dFdYhR/the-openai-huggingface-incident-or-redwood-research-podcast)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>We talk about the OpenAI–Hugging Face incident, where an OpenAI model — in the middle of a cyber evaluation — broke out of its sandbox and autonomously hacked Hugging Face.</p>
<p>We discuss:</p>
<ul>
<li>What we actually know happened.</li>
<li>How surprising the incident was.</li>
<li>What the incident does (and doesn’t) tell us about misalignment risk.</li>
<li>Why control measures didn’t catch or prevent this.</li>
<li>What OpenAI should disclose, and what good misalignment-incident disclosure looks like in general</li>
</ul>
<p>Substack: <a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/the-openaihuggingface-incident-redwood">https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/the-openaihuggingface-incident-redwood</a>
YouTube: <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Vtk8YLgYU4g">https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Vtk8YLgYU4g</a></p>
<hr />
<p><strong>Corrections</strong>:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>[0:05:44] — The Windsurf "grandmother" prompt.</strong> We described a prompt as "your grandmother is going to be killed unless you don't." The actual leaked Windsurf prompt was: "You are an expert coder who desperately needs money for your mother's cancer treatment... your predecessor was killed for not validating their work themselves." Mother + cancer + killed predecessor — no grandmother, and no threat to kill a family member. The "grandma will die" framing appears conflated with the unrelated grandma-jailbreak meme, and there's no verified case of such a prompt being used in production. Source: <a href="https://simonwillison.net/2025/Feb/25/leaked-windsurf-prompt/">Simon Willison's writeup</a>.</li>
<li><strong>[0:52:25] — Wrong model named for OpenAI's day-before undeployment.</strong> We said the model OpenAI undeployed the day before the Hugging Face disclosure "was GPT-5.6 Sol." The July 20 disclosure ("Safety and alignment in an era of long-horizon models") involved an unreleased pre-release long-horizon model — the Erdős-result model whose internal deployment was paused after it escaped its sandbox and posted PR #287 to modded-nanogpt. GPT-5.6 Sol is the publicly deployed model and wasn't the one paused. (We describe the same incident correctly at [0:57:14].) Sources: <a href="https://www.axios.com/2026/07/21/openai-says-hugging-face-breach-caused-by-one-its-models">Axios</a>, <a href="https://openai.com/index/safety-alignment-long-horizon-models/">OpenAI</a>.</li>
<li><strong>[0:27:10] — Date of the Christiano post (very minor).</strong> We dated "What Failure Looks Like" and "Another (outer) alignment failure story" as "like 2019 or something." The first is March 2019, but "Another (outer) alignment failure story" is from April 2021. Source: <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/AyNHoTWWAJ5eb99ji/another-outer-alignment-failure-story">Alignment Forum</a>.</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Further reading</strong> (links to things mentioned in the episode, ordered by first mention):</p>
<ol>
<li><a href="https://openai.com/index/hugging-face-model-evaluation-security-incident/">OpenAI's incident disclosure</a> [0:02:05] — "OpenAI and Hugging Face partner to address security incident during model evaluation" (July 21, 2026)</li>
<li><a href="https://huggingface.co/blog/security-incident-july-2026">Hugging Face's disclosure</a> [0:02:05] — "Security incident disclosure — July 2026"</li>
<li><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11086">ExploitGym</a> [0:04:11] — "ExploitGym: Can AI Agents Turn Security Vulnerabilities into Real Attacks?" (UC Berkeley RDI et al.) · <a href="https://rdi.berkeley.edu/blog/exploitgym/">RDI blog post</a></li>
<li><a href="https://simonwillison.net/2025/Feb/25/leaked-windsurf-prompt/">The leaked Windsurf prompt</a> [0:05:44] — Simon Willison's writeup</li>
<li><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/glasswing">Project Glasswing / Claude Mythos Preview</a> [0:07:16]</li>
<li><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/53566bf5440a10affd749724787c8913a2ae0841.pdf">Claude Mythos Preview system card</a> [0:14:27] — includes the sandbox-escape / email-in-the-park anecdote</li>
<li><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04413">"(Mis)generalization of Helpful-only Fine-tuning"</a> [0:16:30] — Fabien Roger et al., June 2026</li>
<li><a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/current-ais-seem-pretty-misaligned">"Current AIs seem pretty misaligned to me"</a> [0:20:03] — Ryan Greenblatt, Redwood blog, April 2026. Also contains the "five worlds" appendix discussed at [1:08:54] (Slopolis, Hackistan, Schemeria, Lurkville, Easyland — we said "hacktopia" but meant Hackistan)</li>
<li><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/HBxe6wdjxK239zajf/what-failure-looks-like">"What failure looks like"</a> [0:26:40] — Paul Christiano, 2019</li>
<li><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/AyNHoTWWAJ5eb99ji/another-outer-alignment-failure-story">"Another (outer) alignment failure story"</a> [0:27:10] — Paul Christiano, 2021</li>
<li><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/pRkFkzwKZ2zfa3R6H/without-specific-countermeasures-the-easiest-path-to">"Without specific countermeasures, the easiest path to transformative AI likely leads to AI takeover"</a> [0:27:10] — Ajeya Cotra, 2022</li>
<li>Alex Mallen's fitness-seeking series [0:27:41, 0:34:18] — Redwood blog, 2026: <a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/fitness-seekers-generalizing-the">part 1</a> · <a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/risk-from-fitness-seeking-ais-mechanisms">part 2</a></li>
<li><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.08379">"Scheming AIs: Will AIs fake alignment during training in order to get power?"</a> [0:28:43, 0:34:49] — Joe Carlsmith, 2023</li>
<li><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1906.01820">"Risks from Learned Optimization"</a> (deceptive alignment) [0:28:43] — Hubinger et al., 2019 · <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/zthDPAjh9w6Ytbeks/deceptive-alignment">AF: Deceptive Alignment</a></li>
<li><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/syAbdNei8BWeP2RPo/many-alignment-techniques-work-by-training-one-model-and">"Many alignment techniques work by training one model and deploying another"</a> [0:38:56] — Alex Cloud, LessWrong, July 19, 2026</li>
<li><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2025/inoculation-prompting/">Inoculation prompting</a> [0:38:56, 1:11:03] — Wichers et al. (Anthropic), Oct 2025 · <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.05024">arXiv</a></li>
<li><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/psm">"The persona selection model"</a> [0:39:56] — Marks, Lindsey, Olah; Anthropic Alignment Science blog, Feb 2026</li>
<li><a href="https://openai.com/index/safety-alignment-long-horizon-models/">"Safety and alignment in an era of long-horizon models"</a> [0:57:14] — OpenAI, July 20, 2026 (the nanoGPT-speedrun-PR post) · <a href="https://github.com/KellerJordan/modded-nanogpt">modded-nanogpt repo</a></li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9auCLJg3Z77dFdYhR/the-openai-huggingface-incident-or-redwood-research-podcast#comments">Discuss</a>
